* TODO The :blocked: tag writer is org-depend, found after a7f4b40c cleared it by testing the wrong variable name :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       b0c843e3-4252-4e2b-a9e2-2cf91714a1ae
:CREATED:  [2026-09-15 Tue 11:17]
:CATEGORY: Skill
:END:

a7f4b40c investigated a stray :blocked: tag on c44c2119 in August, tested two
candidates in batch, found both clear, and was CANCELLED on the user's report
that the tag was an inadvertent hand-edit. One of those candidates was
org-depend-tag-blocked-TASKS, which is void. The real name is
org-depend-tag-blocked, it is a defcustom defaulting to t, and
org-depend-block-todo ends with (org-toggle-tag "blocked" (if proceed-p 'off
'on)). So org-depend has been the writer all along and the
investigation cleared it on a name typo. Reproduced 2026-09-15: running org-ql's
(blocked) predicate over TODO.org tagged five headings in the live buffer,
unsaved. The tag is maintained in BOTH directions, which satisfies a7f4b40c's
own stated condition for keeping it -- but it is only as fresh as the last
evaluation, and today it encodes a partly-wrong answer because 3f4fd744's
ids(...) wrapper means only middle blocker ids parse. Decide after 3f4fd744
lands: declare it in #+TAGS: and let org-depend maintain it, or set
org-depend-tag-blocked nil.


* TODO Nothing answers who blocks whom; :BLOCKER: is a forward index with no inverse :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       0eecf8e7-a8d5-4da7-9d6d-f397542b2139
:CREATED:  [2026-09-15 Tue 11:10]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:

A :BLOCKER: on X names Y, so "what is X waiting for" is one property read, while
"what is waiting on Y" requires scanning every :BLOCKER: value in both files and
inverting. org_outline already marks [blocked: id ...] per read; the mirror,
[blocks: id ...], would answer the other direction from the same scan. The user
proposed a :blocker: tag alongside a :blocked: one for this, and a tag is the
wrong shape for the reason a7f4b40c cancelled the first: blocked-ness is
derivable and a tag is stored, so it goes stale the moment the last blocker
finishes -- and a pair of tags must be maintained in step with each other AND
with the property, which is three copies of one fact. Derive it per read
instead.


* TODO org_query cannot ask what is blocked, though org-ql has the predicate :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       5acca91b-b7bf-4046-9379-cd9a8f40f116
:CREATED:  [2026-09-15 Tue 11:10]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:

org-ql defines a blocked predicate and org_query does not expose it. Its parser
documents todo:, tags:, priority: and heading:, so a bare "blocked" falls
through to full-text search: measured 2026-09-15 over TODO.org, the sexp
(blocked) returns 10 headings and the plain string returns 26, the difference
being bodies that merely mention the word. Proposed: pass blocked (and its
negation) through, which is the whole change. Worth pairing with a caution in
the tool description, because the derived answer is a union of three mechanisms
-- unfinished children, unchecked checkboxes, and :BLOCKER: via org-depend --
and a reader asking "what is blocked" usually means only the third.


* TODO The attention report renders to a buffer and persists nothing, so every reading is thrown away :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       8070db26-b11a-4008-9fb9-5dcd017d798f
:CREATED:  [2026-09-15 Tue 10:53]
:CATEGORY: Queue
:END:
:PLAN:
*Four questions, and the order is roughly their difficulty.*

**1. Where does an interval live?** Three candidates, each with a real
objection. *On the slice heading* — but a slice carries no clock by
convention, and though an attention interval is not a clock, putting
durations on a grouping re-creates the ambiguity =64d34a64= measured, a
parent's own share indistinguishable from its subtree's. *On the
meta-work datetree's day node* — it is already the place a day's record
gathers, already dated, and =Review and planning= is already half the
label vocabulary; but a slice-labelled interval is not meta-work and
would sit there under a foreign parent. *In its own file* — clean, and
it puts the agenda's view of the human's day outside the tracker, which
may be right or may be exactly the split that makes it get forgotten.

**2. Written, or recomputed on demand?** Recomputation is always
current and always loses the human's corrections, which is the one
thing the derivation cannot supply. Writing keeps them and creates the
idempotency problem =295cde3a= met head-on: a second run must recognise
what it wrote last time rather than duplicate it.

**3. How is an attested interval distinguished from a derived one?**
This is the contract =4f8500e6= settled — *propose what is measured,
ask for what is not* — and it survives only if a reader can tell which
is which after the fact. A corrected interval and a freshly proposed
one must not render identically; a marker is the cheap answer, and
=:CLOSED_SOURCE: git= is the precedent for marking how a value was
derived without building a provenance system.

**4. What happens when the labelled slice closes and archives?**
Intervals attached to a slice travel with it into DONE.org, which may
be right — the record of a day's attention is history — or may put the
agenda's data somewhere the agenda never looks.

*Related and deliberately not merged into this.*
[[id:01849bef-fdc9-4c30-b467-00ca8606e8e0][01849bef]] gives the review buffer a
key for flipping a span's timestamps to active, which is the *manual* path to
the same artifact and belongs to
[[id:6521dd56-8772-416b-9435-6da78bb86b06][6521dd56]]. If both are built, they
should write the same shape to the same place, and whichever lands first sets it
— worth saying now, because the second one landing will otherwise discover the
constraint as a conflict.
:END:

Follow-up to 4f8500e6, which shipped the derivation and deliberately stopped
short of writing anything. M-x claude-code-ide-org-attention-report fills a *org
attention* buffer and that is the whole of its output, so an attested reading
survives only as long as the buffer does and the next run starts from nothing.
Since the intervals are proposals a human corrects against a calendar,
discarding the corrections is the one outcome that makes the whole exercise
pointless.

*No =:BLOCKER:= between this and
[[id:01849bef-fdc9-4c30-b467-00ca8606e8e0][01849bef]], deliberately, and the
reason is worth stating so nobody adds one later.* The relation is a
**coordination constraint, not an ordering**: both write an active interval, so
they must agree on shape and destination, but neither needs the other finished
first and either may land first. A =:BLOCKER:= would assert an order that does
not exist — and pointing them at each other would assert a cycle. Whichever
lands first sets the shape; the second reads what the first wrote.


* TODO org_amend replace=true destroys a slice's member checklist, which is the one thing a slice declares :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       7ee3b71a-b85a-4610-a4ab-b9e06c8d79da
:CREATED:  [2026-09-15 Tue 10:26]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:

Found 2026-09-15 composing 35582d95. replace=true rewrites the body's prose
wholesale, and a slice's Planned: lead and member lines ARE body prose, so
composing a slice in the documented order — add members, then write the body —
silently deletes the membership. The docstring's promise is about drawers
("rewrites only the prose below every drawer, so :PROPERTIES:, :LOGBOOK: and a
:PLAN: drawer cannot be destroyed"), which is true and beside the point: the
checklist is the one thing a slice actually declares, since every other field is
derived. Caught by bin/lint-org, which noticed the :BLOCKER: naming ids that
were no longer members — so the guard worked, but only after the loss. Same
shape as the fill sweep wrapping member lines the same week: a tool rewriting a
region another tool owns, where ownership is invisible in the text. Proposed:
refuse replace=true on a :KIND: slice heading, or preserve the member region
across the rewrite the way the drawers are preserved.

*Not a regression from [[id:afea7e4f-41b3-4eda-83f1-39e1b9e7a61a][afea7e4f]],
checked rather than assumed (the user's question, 2026-09-15).* =replace==
arrived in =28d68db= on 2026-08-26, two weeks before that slice existed; both
=claude-code-ide-org-amend= and =--replace-body= are **byte-identical between
=origin/main= and the slice's HEAD**; and the one branch commit naming
=--slice-member-regexp= (=e8f18e0=) only *reads* it, in the new
=--generated-line-p= predicate. The defect is latent since the revision mode
shipped.

*What changed was practice, not code.* =replace== exists for a body that
has become a transcript, which is a *task* heading accumulating
corrections; a slice's body is written once at composition, so the
combination had never arisen. On 2026-09-14 the user asked for
=295cde3a='s forty paragraphs to be compressed, =replace== was the right
tool for that, and hours later the same move was reached for while
composing a slice. A habit formed on tasks migrated to slices inside one
session — which is how a latent defect gets found, and worth recording
because no code change would have predicted it.

*Two defects this week share a shape, and the pair is the argument for how
to fix this one.* The fill sweep wrapped member lines (=496b665e=) and
=replace== erased them (here). **Neither was a code regression.** Both
were existing writers meeting slice member lines for the first time,
because a member line *looks like ordinary body prose* and nothing in the
text says it is owned. =bin/lint-org= caught both — after the loss.

*So the durable fix is not a fourth special case in a fourth writer.* It
is that the member region should be identifiable to **any** writer, which
is what =--generated-line-p= started doing for exactly one of them.
Teaching =org_amend= to refuse =replace== on a =:KIND: slice= heading
closes today's hole and leaves the next writer to rediscover it.

*And the guard that caught this was luck rather than design.* The error
came from the =:BLOCKER:= naming ids that were no longer members — so it
fired only because the slice *had* a blocker. A slice whose members are
all finished carries no =:BLOCKER:= at all (=0086614a= shrinks it to
nothing), so destroying its checklist would leave cookie and blocker
agreeing at empty and nothing to object. The conventions already record
that exact shape as the worst direction to fail in: a slice whose members
cannot be found "reports =[0/0]= as though it were empty rather than
broken".

*Until this lands, the mitigation is a practice and should be stated as
one:* do not call =org_amend= with =replace== on a slice. Compose the body
first and add members after, or amend rather than replace.


* NEXT [0/4] The plugin ships to a second repo unenforced, uncategorised and over-claimed :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       35582d95-003a-4735-9b19-36f7ad813f0d
:CREATED:  [2026-09-15 Tue 10:08]
:KIND:     slice
:COOKIE_DATA: checkbox recursive
:CATEGORY: Slices
:BLOCKER:  3f4fd744-4ba7-4be8-9fe4-54e46119515a b0d55552-6bbf-45b8-9897-f62e6bd779e3 784d80c5-8750-4ebe-b5e5-c213fc809d80 af2f345e-b7db-4025-9f63-c5fce09fdd8a
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "NEXT"       from "TODO"       [2026-09-15 Tue 10:48] \\
  first of the four sequenced in next-session.md
:END:
:PLAN:
*Why these four and not the other three.* The euchre blocking set named on
[[id:6a207b00-8fa1-441e-8ee5-354702bc979b][6a207b00]] has seven candidates.
Three already belong to other slices — =965f94eb= and =5e731a23= to
[[id:8a2eb687-28da-4d44-893b-db59298bdc5b][8a2eb687]], =c9940558= to
[[id:749301a0-0bd0-4fbd-b3f7-79739226e15a][749301a0]] — and a member counted by
two slices makes two cookies disagree about the same work, so those stay where
they are and are cross-referenced rather than adopted. This slice takes the four
that belonged to no slice at all, which is also why the set is coherent: they
are what a *second* repo meets and this one never does, because this repo grew
each convention alongside its code and a second one receives it finished.

*The order, and the reasoning.* Correctness first, then capture, then the
two install-time defects.

1. =3f4fd744= is the sharpest and goes first for a second reason beyond
   severity: **8 of 12 =:BLOCKER:= properties currently enforce
   nothing**, and every blocker written *during* this slice's own work
   would inherit the broken syntax if it were fixed last.
2. =b0d55552= next, because everything captured after it is categorised
   and everything before it is not — a fresh tracker's whole history
   turns on when this lands.
3. =784d80c5= and =af2f345e= are install-time and independent of each
   other, so they sort by encounter order rather than by dependency.

*The standing hazard this slice exists to close.* =3f4fd744= is a convention
that *looks* like enforcement and is not — the same failure shape as everything
in [[id:8a2eb687-28da-4d44-893b-db59298bdc5b][8a2eb687]], and already deployed
in euchre since 2026-09-11. A mechanism whose removal is invisible is the wrong
mechanism; one that never fired and never said so is worse.

*It is the first of four, sequenced on
[[file:~/.claude/commands/next-session.md][next-session.md]]*: this,
then =749301a0=, then =8a2eb687=, then =6521dd56=, serially and each
with its own pull request. Four concurrent branches were considered and
declined — measured 2026-09-15, **200 of the last 200 code commits touch
=config.el=**, one 17,000-line file, so parallel branches would conflict
in proportion to the work and the merges would cost exactly the attended
time the plan is trying to save.
:END:

The plugin's conventions arrive in a consuming repo unenforced and its
captures uncategorised, and its installer over-claims what it owns.
These are the four euchre-adoption blockers that belong to no other
slice — each something a second repo meets on day one and this one never
does. Proposed: fix the enforcement defect first so later work inherits
a working =:BLOCKER:=, then capture's missing =:CATEGORY:=, then the two
install-time defects.

Planned:

- [ ] [[id:b0d55552-6bbf-45b8-9897-f62e6bd779e3][b0d55552]] TODO org_capture cannot set =:CATEGORY:=, so every captured heading is uncategorised and nothing notices
- [ ] [[id:784d80c5-8750-4ebe-b5e5-c213fc809d80][784d80c5]] TODO claude-org-setup --org scaffolds or appends the consuming repo's .gitignore
- [ ] [[id:af2f345e-b7db-4025-9f63-c5fce09fdd8a][af2f345e]] TODO The glue claims ownership of the whole wiring block, but the 202 override ships nowhere

  defined the slice and sequenced the four

- [[orgit-rev:claude-code-ide-org::4a62bf9][4a62bf9]] [2026-09-15 Tue 13:0x]
  three members closed, the fourth WAITING on a posed decision; PR #24 opened on =feature/plugin-day-one=

Commits 7 against 50 (two of the seven are corpus and bookkeeping). Review findings: none yet, the PR being unreviewed; =afea7e4f= had 0.64 per member. * TODO A bookkeeping burst reads as many headings worked at once, and inflates every window it lands in :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       369ad62a-e2ce-4cab-b2ee-0e83ad24f249
:CREATED:  [2026-09-14 Mon 17:16]
:CATEGORY: Queue
:END:

Residue from 295cde3a. The window allocator counts a heading as worked when a
write tool named it, and org_capture had to leave that list because a filing
burst creates a dozen headings inside one window and read as a dozen headings
being worked — 15 in the worst window on 2026-09-10. The same shape survives in
org_set_property and org_slice_add_member, which touch many headings during
slice composition. Proposed: decide whether a bookkeeping write on a heading is
work *on* that heading, and if not, split --worked-tool-names into work and
bookkeeping so a composition pass lands on review and planning whole rather than
a minute each across the headings it filed.


* TODO Make =d= mark for dismissal rather than dismiss, so a shared reason can be given once :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       ee3c22c9-f99c-4d41-9c3d-8f39af916fff
:CREATED:  [2026-09-14 Mon 12:15]
:CATEGORY: Apply
:END:

Asked by the user 2026-09-14, immediately after dismissing seven items that
shared one reason: =d= should leave a mark the way =m= does — a *different*
mark, =D= suggested — so dismissal becomes a bulk operation instead of seven
separate acts. The motivating case is concrete: all seven were state transitions
that had already landed, dismissed for the identical reason, and the pass had no
way to say that once. Dired is the precedent the review buffer already follows
for =m=/=u=/=t=/=U=, and dired marks for deletion with =d= and executes with =x=
— so this would bring =d= into the same idiom rather than inventing one, and =x=
already means "act on what is marked" here. Worth deciding together: whether =x=
then executes both marks (apply the =m=s, dismiss the =D=s) or whether dismissal
needs its own execute key; whether a =D= mark can carry a typed reason once for
the whole set, which is the part that would actually have saved work; and how
=D= interacts with the auto-marking =b6e229c7= does, since an item arriving
pre-marked =m= and then marked =D= must not be both. Supersedes the narrower
cursor question in [[id:e95cc5d1-f8ae-4aad-b9cd-5bd0277e1d4b][e95cc5d1]] if it
ships — a mark does not remove its line, so the advance becomes correct by
construction — but that heading stands on its own until then, since it is a live
defect and this is a design.


* TODO =d= advances point in the review buffer, onto whatever moved up to replace the dismissed item :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       e95cc5d1-f8ae-4aad-b9cd-5bd0277e1d4b
:CREATED:  [2026-09-14 Mon 12:15]
:CATEGORY: Apply
:END:

Reported by the user 2026-09-14 after dismissing seven stale state items in one
pass. =claude-code-ide-org-review-dismiss= advances point the way =m= does, but
the two are not analogous: =m= leaves its item in place, so advancing lands on
the *next* item, whereas =d= removes its item, so the next one moves *up into
the vacated line* — and advancing then skips it. The user must move back by hand
after every dismissal, which is exactly the friction =m='s advance exists to
remove, inverted. Proposed: =d= leaves point where it is, since the line now
holds the item that would otherwise have been skipped. Note this is a different
question from where point lands when the *list* is rebuilt (=9e80a32d=): nothing
is rebuilt here, one line is removed from a rendered buffer.


* TODO Nothing fills prose on write, so body text arrives as one long line and the human reflows it by hand :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       b52df20b-45c7-41aa-9d77-44ccf06c647f
:CREATED:  [2026-09-14 Mon 10:30]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:

Reported by the user 2026-09-14: new headings arrive with single-line bodies and
are being reflowed with =M-q= by hand. Measured rather than assumed — **nothing
in =config.el= fills anything**: no =fill-region=, =fill-paragraph=, =auto-fill=
or =fill-column= anywhere, so =org_capture='s note and =org_amend='s text are
written exactly as sent. The wrapping in older bodies was therefore never a
feature; it came from sessions happening to embed newlines in the text they
sent, and it has decayed as that habit did. Across TODO.org by heading creation
month, the median *longest* prose line runs 70 (July), 186 (August), 257
(September), with the share of headings carrying a line over 300 characters
going 20% → 34% → 46% — a steady drift, not a break, and understated at the
recent end because the user has already reflowed the newest bodies. Note the
tempting diagnosis is wrong: =204d1b0a= (2026-09-11) made =org_capture= stop
discarding its note, which looks like the genesis and is not — =org_amend= wrote
verbatim throughout and the trend predates it by two months. Proposed: fill in
the *write* path, so the property holds regardless of who or what is sending the
text — the project's own precedent is that a rule depending on being remembered
is the wrong rule, and "the agent should wrap its prose" is exactly that.
=org-fill-paragraph= already knows not to touch tables, blocks and links, so the
mechanism is org's rather than ours; what needs deciding is the column and
whether a body that arrives *already* wrapped is left alone.


* WAITING Adopt the plugin in agentic-euchre, so the two projects stop being worked in parallel :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       6a207b00-8fa1-441e-8ee5-354702bc979b
:CREATED:  [2026-09-11 Fri 20:05]
:CATEGORY: Dev
:BLOCKER:  35582d95-003a-4735-9b19-36f7ad813f0d 8a2eb687-28da-4d44-893b-db59298bdc5b 749301a0-0bd0-4fbd-b3f7-79739226e15a 6521dd56-8772-416b-9435-6da78bb86b06
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "WAITING"    from "TODO"       [2026-09-15 Tue 10:09] \\
  blocked on four slices: 35582d95, 8a2eb687, 749301a0, 6521dd56
:END:

Stated by the user 2026-09-11 and previously unrecorded anywhere: "a number of
things unresolved here are preventing me from applying this plugin and its
conventions to the euchre project rather than working the two of them in
parallel." That makes euchre adoption a *goal with a blocking set*, not an
eventual nice-to-have, and it reframes priority — every item in that set is
holding a second project in a worse workflow every day it stays open, which is a
cost nothing in the tracker currently expresses. Candidates, offered for the
user to confirm or correct rather than assumed: =965f94eb= (every standalone
client shares one project context, chosen by unstable ordering), =5e731a23= (a
deferred targetless capture loses its project routing at apply), =c9940558=
(guidepost aggregation ignores =cwd=, so two projects' turns cluster into one
span), =3f4fd744= (=:BLOCKER:= would ship to euchre enforcing nothing),
=b0d55552= (=org_capture= cannot set =:CATEGORY:=, so a fresh tracker's headings
arrive uncategorised), =784d80c5= (=claude-org-setup --org= and the consuming
repo's =.gitignore=) and =af2f345e= (the glue claims the whole wiring block).
Note =fbaf8009= and =d718f6b6= are already closed and were both multi-project
defects, so the set is smaller than it was. **The =:BLOCKER:= is deliberately
unset until the user names the real list** — a blocker invented from a
plausible-looking survey is exactly the kind that reads as enforcement and
enforces the wrong thing.

*Adoption step added 2026-09-15 from =3f4fd744=:* any =:BLOCKER:= euchre wrote before the plugin's 5f7ecdb carries the =ids(...)= wrapper and enforces nothing under org-depend. One run of =M-x claude-code-ide-org-normalize-blocker-syntax= there (prefix argument to write) rewrites them; its =bin/lint-org= will name each one until then.

*Correction, same day:* measured rather than assumed — euchre's TODO.org and DONE.org carry no =:BLOCKER:= property at all, and neither file is in the live Emacs's tracked set (=claude-code-ide-org--tracked-files= names none of them), so there is nothing for the normaliser to do there. The step above is dormant: any blocker euchre writes from now on comes out bare, since the plugin's own write sites do. If one ever needs it, =M-x claude-code-ide-org-normalize-blocker-syntax= runs over whatever files that Emacs tracks — bare =M-x= is a dry run, =C-u= writes.




* TODO Unplanned work in flight belongs to no slice: planned is writable, incidental is closed-only :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       9bab114d-44fa-488e-92bd-ff7c3fd8065d
:CREATED:  [2026-09-11 Fri 19:27]
:CATEGORY: Slices
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-09-14 Mon 12:44]--[2026-09-14 Mon 12:46] =>  0:02
- [2026-09-14 Mon 12:44]--[2026-09-14 Mon 12:46] assigned at review
:END:

Work has three states relative to an open slice and only two can be represented.
Planned-and-open is a member line; unplanned-and-*closed* is picked up
automatically, since =--slice-incidental-ids= defines an incidental as "a
heading whose close this slice owns" and is derived from closes, never curated
(=58e6c6a0=, deliberately — a hand-kept list goes stale by the second day). But
unplanned-and-*still-open* appears nowhere: not planned, because it wasn't; not
incidental, because it hasn't closed. The consequence is the sharp part — such
work cannot enter the derived =:BLOCKER:=, so a slice can reach =DONE= while
work it caused is still open, which is =e80f6ccf='s class arriving from a second
direction. Note what is *not* wrong: nothing forbids adding a member to an open
slice — =org_slice_add_member= refuses only a CLOSED one — so the sanctioned
move already exists, and the conventions already say review residue and
incidental work "both become member lines, so the distinction lives in the
debrief rather than in the list". What misleads is the vocabulary: =Planned:= is
a structural anchor for the member parser (=a43cfaa0=), not a claim of
foresight, and reading it as foresight makes the one writable list look
unavailable for the very work that most needs it. Proposed: say so explicitly in
the conventions — a mid-flight addition is a *declaration of membership*, not a
retroactive claim to have foreseen it — and decide whether in-flight unplanned
work should be marked as such at all, or left to the debrief as the
review-residue rule already does. Raised by the user 2026-09-11 as "a stark
inconsistency we should not abide".

*The user's ruling, 2026-09-11, which settles the open question above and
inverts the current default:* "issues that are **discovered** during the course
of a slice are already by nature incidental. unless there is a reason to defer
them, they **should** become a member, albeit unplanned, of the slice, because
they have some bearing on achieving the desired end state of the slice."

*What that changes.* An incidental is currently defined by its **close** —
=--slice-incidental-ids= lists "a heading whose close this slice owns" — so
unplanned work is adopted retroactively, only if it finishes inside the window,
and never blocks anything. Under this ruling adoption happens at **discovery**,
which is the only version that puts the issue into the derived =:BLOCKER:= and
therefore stops the slice closing over it. Default becomes adopt; deferral
becomes the exception that needs a stated reason. Mechanically it needs no new
tool: =org_slice_add_member= already accepts an open slice, and
=--slice-incidental-ids= excludes ids the slice already names, so an adopted
member is not double-listed when it later closes.

*The test that bounds it, and it is in the ruling's own "because" clause.* Read
as a justification, "they have some bearing on achieving the desired end state"
explains why adopt is the right default; read as a **test**, it is what keeps a
slice from sprawling. The session that raised this is the counterexample:
working =afea7e4f= produced five discoveries (=c9940558=, =2deb090f=,
=b57c7515=, =3f4fd744=, =f1e36a65=), every one of them incidental by discovery
and *none* bearing on the transcript join, which shipped complete without them.
Adopting all five would have left that slice at =[0/9]=, unable to close, held
open by three unrelated subsystems. So "no bearing on this slice's end state" is
a legitimate reason to defer — and on this evidence it is the common case, not
the rare one.

*Which leaves one genuinely open sub-question:* whether an adopted-unplanned
member is *visibly* distinguished from a planned one. The conventions' existing
answer for review residue is that both become member lines and the distinction
lives in the debrief; the ruling's phrase "a member, albeit unplanned" may want
more than that. A third parsed lead (=Adopted:=, beside =Planned:= and
=Incidental:=) is the obvious shape but is a real parser change — the member
scan bounds itself on those leads — so it should be decided rather than assumed.

*Sharpened by the user, same day, and it corrects the paragraph above.*
"Relationship" is the right word, not "bearing" — the sentiment to make
matter-of-course is: **we understand it now, it is in context, the costs and
risks are low, so we might as well take it on and be done with it.** Expected to
be the rule rather than the exception: if feature A is landing shortly, the
feature H that surfaced while working A — or while looking ahead to B through G
— is very likely wanted too.

*My counterexample was overstated and is withdrawn in part.* It tested the
narrow end state ("does the transcript join need this to ship?") rather than
relationship to the work in hand. Re-read under the looser test, three or four
of the session's five discoveries qualify: =c9940558= was discovered *by* the
tracker annotation and lives in the same render path, and =b57c7515= bears on
the join directly — the prompt lookup keys on =session_id=, and a re-key leaves
two transcripts for one conversation. Only =3f4fd744= is genuinely unrelated. So
"no bearing" is not the common case; the marginal cost of adjacent work while
the context is already loaded really is the dominant term.

*What survives is not a filter on adoption but the absence of a stopping point.*
If adoption is the rule, membership grows monotonically while the slice runs,
and every adopted member can itself produce discoveries. The termination
condition is then not "the work ran out" but "we decided to stop adopting" — and
nothing in the conventions says when that is. That, rather than over-adoption,
is what turns the sentiment into scope creep: the two are distinguished by the
*existence of a closing gate*, not by the decision to take something on.

*Proposed gate, because it costs nothing and reuses a line that already exists:*
**adoption is open while the slice is =DOING=; entering =REVIEW= closes it.**
=REVIEW= on a grouping already means every member terminal and the work not yet
integrated (=e80f6ccf=), so it is exactly the moment membership should stop
moving. A discovery after that goes to the next slice or stands alone — which is
also what gives the *next* slice its natural seed.

*One legibility consequence to expect rather than be surprised by.* Adopting at
discovery inflates the cookie's denominator before its numerator, so a slice's
progress visibly regresses — =[3/6]= becomes =[3/9]=. Incidentals never did
this, being done on arrival. It is honest and it will read as a defect to anyone
who has not been told, so it belongs in the convention text.

*Approved as a trial, 2026-09-11:* run the depth-one rule on the slice in hand
"and one or two more to see how it affects us". So it stays **provisional** in
the conventions rather than hardening — the exit condition is two or three
slices' experience, and what to look for is whether adoption actually stops at
depth one or whether the distinction between a planned member's discovery and an
adopted member's discovery turns out to be unreadable in the moment.

*The user's own risk analysis, which is the part that makes the trial cheap:*
"the worst that can happen is that future open slices (not =DOING=) get
=CANCELLED= or entirely reformulated — same effect once a new slice with any
members that were in a =CANCELLED= slice is created." That is right, and it
names a release valve the conventions had not: **cancelling and reformulating an
open slice is a normal move, not a failure.** A =CANCELLED= slice keeps its
body, which the composition section already says is usually the part worth
keeping, and its members are free to be composed into a successor. The cost of
an over-adopted slice is therefore bounded by one reformulation.

*And that valve has a named trigger:* "that should always be an option when
feature Q is suddenly needed so that work on another project can proceed". Not
hypothetical — see [[id:6a207b00-8fa1-441e-8ee5-354702bc979b][6a207b00]]:
adopting the plugin in agentic-euchre is blocked by unresolved work here, and a
slice standing between that and the user is a slice worth reformulating rather
than finishing out of order.

*The user, 2026-09-14, correcting this heading's own framing and mine:* "I
thought *incidental* means *not planned*, supported by absence from the
operative version(s) of =next-session.md=. Otherwise, what's the meaning of the
=Planned:= marker?"

*That supplies an objective test the conventions did not have, and it is
checkable in one command.* Measured against =afea7e4f= the same day: =325679af=,
=96ddf1ef=, =295cde3a= and =fbaf8009= each appear two or three times in the
operative =next-session.md=; =f1e36a65=, =f3a53bd4=, =40159d43=, =b23e5bcc= and
=169df26b= appear **zero** times. So five of nine members sat under =Planned:=
having never been planned — including =f1e36a65=, adopted hours before the other
four and mislabelled the same way.

*Which retires the claim recorded above that =Planned:= is "a structural anchor
for the member parser, not an assertion of foresight".* That was read off
=a43cfaa0='s title — "member anchor, insertion point, self-heal" — three
*structural* jobs, from which semantic emptiness does not follow. A lead that
were only an anchor would read =Members:=. It reads =Planned:=, and the plan
file is what makes the word verifiable rather than decorative.

*So adoption must not write into the planned list*, and the asymmetry this
heading names is sharper than first stated: the writable list is the one whose
label adoption falsifies, and the correctly-labelled list is derived-only. Using
the first is not a workaround, it is a false statement about what the slice
foresaw.

*Proposed mechanism, mirroring =:DROPPED:= exactly rather than inventing a
shape.* A =:ADOPTED:= property on the slice naming ids declared incidental at
discovery, with the refresh rendering the =Incidental:= section as the **union**
of those declared ids and the ones derived from closes. That keeps both section
names meaning what they say, makes "incidental" writable at the moment of
discovery — which is what "see them accumulate along the way" needs — and
follows the project's standing pattern of declaring the exception and deriving
the rendering. It also subsumes the third-lead idea floated earlier: no new
section is needed, because =Incidental:= already means the right thing and only
lacked a way to be written.

*Until it exists, the five sit under the wrong lead and the slice's body says
so*, which is the honest interim: visible and wrong beats invisible.

*Declined by the user, 2026-09-14, and the reasoning retires the proposal above
rather than deferring it:* "I'm inclined to move the other way and just allow
the slice to grow according to convention without regard for membership. If
anyone ever cares to discriminate then the grep can happen on demand. Another
case of over-regulation on my part without a clear necessity."

*That is the better call, and it follows this project's own precedent more
closely than the =:ADOPTED:= proposal did.* The planned/adopted distinction is
**derivable** — the plan file says who was planned — so storing it would be a
second copy of a fact that already exists, and the project's standing objection
to derived-facts-stored (=a7f4b40c=) applies exactly. =:DROPPED:= earned its
property because a bare cookie deletion was genuinely *ambiguous* between three
readings (=1b727475=); nothing is ambiguous here. And thirteen slices have run
without anyone once needing the distinction at a glance, which is the
demonstrated-premise test =7771fc63= set.

*So the =Planned:= lead keeps its name and a slice simply grows.* Its literal
accuracy for adopted members is the price, and it is a small one against a
property, a refresh branch, a lint rule and a convention section — none of which
anything has asked for.

*One prerequisite had to be met for this to be safe, and it was not.* The grep
is only durable while the operative plan version stays pinned, and
=next-session.md= is rewritten in place. =afea7e4f= carried **no** =orgit-rev=
revision link at all — three days into the slice — so the discrimination was
derivable from the *current* file and would have become guesswork the moment the
next slice rewrote it. The link is now on the slice. **The convention that
matters here is therefore not a new property but the existing one: link the plan
revision when the slice is picked up**, and this is its first recorded failure.


* TODO org_capture cannot set =:CATEGORY:=, so every captured heading is uncategorised and nothing notices :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       b0d55552-6bbf-45b8-9897-f62e6bd779e3
:CREATED:  [2026-09-11 Fri 18:59]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:
:DEBRIEF:
Shipped with one decision the body had deferred — required rather than defaulted, at top level only — and one the body had not anticipated: a value new to the file warns instead of refusing, because the plugin ships to repos whose taxonomies this module cannot know. The lint half was smaller than planned: the rule and its drawer-as-text reader already existed as a warning, so the work was the severity, its comment, and the pre-commit test fixture that the escalation broke the same day (the fixture's "clean" task had no category — the exact lag the fixture's own comment records from 2026-08-27). Verified: 662 of 662 in the suite, the hook test's new category cases, the live tool refusing a top-level capture and listing nine values in use, and the mutation test on a corpus copy. Measured: 0 of 107 before and after — the fix's job was to hold the number, and the discipline had already repaired it by hand. One precondition worth stating for the next session: the MCP client caches a tool's argument schema at connect, so passing =category= through =org_capture= from an already-running Claude Code session may need that session restarted; the refusal path needs nothing, since it takes no new argument. Nothing left undone.
:END:
:PLAN:

=org_capture= takes title, note, tags, target and initial_state — there is no
category argument — so a heading created through the tool carries no
=:CATEGORY:= and cannot be given one at creation. Since =29439196= retired the
level-1 category tier, a *top-level* capture therefore has no category at all,
and =org-get-category= falls back to the **file name**: measured 2026-09-11,
three headings captured that day resolve to the category ="TODO"=, rendering an
agenda line as =TODO TODO Some task= — exactly the keyword/category collision
the conventions banned ="Review"= to avoid. Nothing catches it: =bin/lint-org=
does not mention CATEGORY once. The omission hides because =:CATEGORY:=
*inherits*, so a capture later refiled under a parent silently acquires one and
looks correct, which is why this went unnoticed until a top-level capture was
inspected directly. Proposed: give =org_capture= a =category= argument, and have
=bin/lint-org= report a top-level heading with no drawer value as an error —
read from the drawer, never =org-entry-get=, which computes the very fallback
that makes the defect invisible. Worth deciding at the same time whether a
missing category should default from the target's parent or simply be required,
since the capture reply already names where the heading landed.

*Measured 2026-09-11 at the user's question, and the onset is sharp rather than
gradual.* Over the 111 level-1 headings in TODO.org — read from each heading's
**own property drawer** via =org-map-entries=, never =org-entry-get=, which
computes the fallback this defect hides behind — **10 carried no =:CATEGORY:=**,
and every one of them was created on 2026-09-10 or 2026-09-11. July (5) and
August (46) are clean; so are the first nine days of September. By day: 09-09
two created and none missing, 09-10 three of thirteen, 09-11 seven of
twenty-four.

*Intermittent rather than total, which is the signature of the actual cause.*
The missing capability in =org_capture= has been constant since the tool
existed, so it cannot explain a change of behaviour on one day. What changed is
where the taxonomy is *written down*. Commit =42a3221= (2026-09-09, the plugin
packaging) moved the ten =:CATEGORY:= values out of =CLAUDE.md=, which loads in
**every** session, into the new =.claude/rules/org-conventions-local.md=, which
carries =paths: ["**/*.org"]= and therefore loads **only when an =.org= file is
in play**. The first uncategorised capture is the next day. Sessions that opened
an org file kept setting categories; sessions working purely through the MCP
tools — which is how a heading is normally captured — no longer saw the list at
all.

*The conventions predicted this in their own first paragraph:* "a path-scoped
rule that does not load is a rule that does not apply, and the failure is
silent". Capturing a heading is exactly a thing done with no =.org= file open,
so the taxonomy is on the wrong side of the line that paragraph draws.

*So the fix has two halves and the order matters.* **Move the =:CATEGORY:=
taxonomy to an always-loaded rule** — that is the root cause, and it repairs the
discipline for humans and agents alike without any code. Then **give
=org_capture= a =category= argument and =bin/lint-org= a rule**, so the
discipline does not depend on being remembered at all. The user's framing
settles the lint's scope: inheritance is the right answer for a child, and a
*first-degree* heading must carry its own, so the rule is "a level-1 heading
with no drawer-local value is an error".

*Repaired in passing, not deferred:* categories were assigned by hand to the
eight real headings (=165ce65a=, =204d1b0a=, =b112878e=, =489d6f61=, =325679af=,
=784d80c5=, =fbaf8009=, =af2f345e=). The two that remain uncategorised are
cancelled scratch probes.

*The measurement trap is wider than this body says, found 2026-09-15 by
falling into it twice in a row.* The warning above names
=org-entry-get=; **=(org-entry-properties nil 'standard)= computes the
same fallback**, despite =standard= sounding like "the drawer's own".
Both reported *zero* level-1 headings without a =:CATEGORY:= while
=369ad62a= plainly had none — its drawer holds =:ID:= and =:CREATED:=
and nothing else. Only reading the property drawer as **text** found it.

That matters for the lint rule this heading proposes, because the rule
will be written in elisp by someone reaching for the obvious accessor,
and a check that cannot fail is worse than no check — which is
=542924c1='s subject, already closed in this slice's own theme.

*So the rule's implementation needs a discriminating test*: a fixture
heading with no =:CATEGORY:= in its drawer, asserted to be *reported*.
Written against either accessor above, that test passes while the check
does nothing.

*One heading was still uncategorised at this writing* — =369ad62a=,
captured 2026-09-14 — and has been given =Queue=. The count is now 0 of
103, which is the number the fix has to hold rather than reach.

*The miss rate measured on a live session, 2026-09-15, and the
conditions matter more than the figure.* Five headings were captured
during the session that composed =35582d95=: =369ad62a=, =496b665e=,
=35582d95=, =7ee3b71a= and =8070db26=. Four carried a =:CATEGORY:=
because the agent set one by hand immediately after each capture; one
did not, and was found only by a loose-ends sweep the user happened to
ask for. **One in five, and that is the optimistic case** — the agent
had read this heading's body that same session and was primed by it. A
session that has not read it has no such reflex, which is the whole
point: the taxonomy is on the wrong side of a path-scoped rule.

*Recorded as an argument against hotfixing it out of sequence rather
than for it.* The damage is a missing property — repairable by a text
grep, as that sweep was — not lost data, and this is member two of the
slice nominated to start next. Working it early would take half the
fix (the prose move is explicitly ordered first) outside the slice that
counts it, leaving the cookie lying about what remains.

:END:
*Resolved 2026-09-15* (prose [[orgit-rev:claude-code-ide-org::a2f770a][a2f770a]], code [[orgit-rev:claude-code-ide-org::bb8f3af][bb8f3af]], second member of =35582d95=). Root cause first: the ten values now live in =.claude/rules/org-categories.md= with no =paths:= scope, so every session loads them; the local conventions file keeps the history and points at the move. Then the mechanism: =org_capture= takes =category=, written into the drawer after =:CREATED:= on both the immediate and the deferred path (the hook maps it, the parser and apply carry it). The decision the body left open went to *required* — a top-level capture without one is refused, and the refusal names the values the file already uses; under an =:ID:= target the child inherits and nothing is required. A value the file has never used is written with a warning, not refused, since the taxonomy is the consuming repo's own. The lint's level-1 rule went from warn to error; it already read the drawer as text, which is why only the severity changed. Before: 107 level-1 headings, 0 missing. After: 107, 0 — the number held, and the mutation test (one =:CATEGORY:= line deleted from a copy) proves the error names the heading.



* TODO [0/7] A span is only as true as its owner, its edges and its payload :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       749301a0-0bd0-4fbd-b3f7-79739226e15a
:CREATED:  [2026-09-11 Fri 18:58]
:CATEGORY: Slices
:KIND:     slice
:COOKIE_DATA: checkbox recursive
:BLOCKER:  406e9200-c3e7-49ac-a8d0-d6ff8e061ce4 b57c7515-c511-4198-b311-26cefc52a60d 9202b39d-49e4-441c-89f2-382feb4ef1ea 7d739afd-f879-445c-b0b6-65e295585a94 c9940558-b23e-4b2c-86e8-e75a377634aa c54c4215-90e3-4e0c-9de1-990c0406a2b2 2deb090f-593d-40e3-9044-fda318d9b6de
:END:
:PLAN:
*Why this order.* Each group hands its output to the next, so working them out
of sequence means re-doing the later ones. **Ownership first** — until a stretch
of events has one owner, no boundary rule can be evaluated, because which events
a rule *sees* depends on how lanes are keyed. =b57c7515= leads rather than
=9202b39d= even though the latter is older and already carries a measured rule:
=b57c7515= poses the question that decides whether that rule is the right
*shape* ("per pairing function, or once where lanes are formed"), and
=9202b39d='s leading-=pause= test demonstrably misses the second shape. Answer
the shape question, then implement. =7d739afd= closes the group, since
union-not-sum is the arithmetic that only makes sense once lanes are settled.

**Boundaries second.** =c9940558= and =c54c4215= both decide where a span may
divide — on a project change and around an exclusion respectively — and both
operate on whatever the ownership work leaves them.

**Payload last.** =2deb090f= is what actually reaches the =:LOGBOOK:=, and it is
the only member whose correct answer depends on all five others: a rule for what
a 0-run span writes is worth nothing while the span's extent is still moving.

*What this slice is not.* It does not reopen =406e9200='s finished ten, and it
adds no new work — every member already existed as a child of that story. The
story keeps its own record and its cookie; this only sequences the residue and
gives it an integration point, which is the thing a story cannot do.

*Not started.* Composed open, as its own proposal, with no =next-session.md=
revision link — that link arrives when a slice is actually picked up.
:END:

The six open children remaining under =406e9200= fail in three distinct places
rather than one: who *owns* a stretch of events (=b57c7515=, =9202b39d=,
=7d739afd=), where a span is allowed to *divide* (=c9940558=, =c54c4215=), and
what finally *lands* in the drawer (=2deb090f=). None is independent of the
others — a change to lane identity alters which events a boundary rule even
sees, and a boundary change alters what the writer is handed. Proposed: take
them in that order, as one integration point, so the pipeline is corrected in a
single direction of travel rather than three overlapping ones. Composed
2026-09-11 at the user's motion, as the story's open residue; the story keeps
its finished ten and this sequences what is left.

Planned:

- [-] [[id:406e9200-c3e7-49ac-a8d0-d6ff8e061ce4][406e9200]] DOING Make the guidepost pipeline produce durations that mean what they say
  - [ ] [[id:b57c7515-c511-4198-b311-26cefc52a60d][b57c7515]] NEXT A session id that changes mid-work splits one clock bracket across two queue files
  - [ ] [[id:9202b39d-49e4-441c-89f2-382feb4ef1ea][9202b39d]] TODO A background job's first turn is bracketed across two session_ids
  - [ ] [[id:7d739afd-f879-445c-b0b6-65e295585a94][7d739afd]] TODO Concurrent sessions' turns must union, not sum, wherever time is totalled
  - [ ] [[id:c9940558-b23e-4b2c-86e8-e75a377634aa][c9940558]] TODO Guidepost aggregation ignores =cwd=, so two projects' turns cluster into one span
  - [ ] [[id:c54c4215-90e3-4e0c-9de1-990c0406a2b2][c54c4215]] TODO A split span drops the unowned gaps between the clock pairs that split it
  - [ ] [[id:2deb090f-593d-40e3-9044-fda318d9b6de][2deb090f]] TODO Accepting a 0-run span records none of its minutes, and only =e= can claim them


* TODO =:BLOCKER: ids(...)= is org-edna syntax; org-depend cannot parse it, so 8 of 12 blockers enforce nothing :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       3f4fd744-4ba7-4be8-9fe4-54e46119515a
:CREATED:  [2026-09-11 Fri 18:08]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:
:DEBRIEF:
Shipped as proposed; the one design choice was where to close the read/enforce divergence, and it went to the lint rather than the readers — a strict reader would make a consuming repo's not-yet-normalised corpus read as dependency-free, which is the original defect in the other direction. Verified: the org-depend test red against HEAD at the discriminating assertion, green after; the lint rule fired 35 times on the real corpus before the rewrite and 0 after, which is the mutation evidence that it reaches the real path; the full suite 657 of 657. Measured: 35 of 39 corpus properties carried the wrapper (13 in TODO.org, 22 in DONE.org), not the "8 of 12" in the title — that figure counted TODO.org's unenforced ones only. Residue: a consuming repo that received the plugin before this fix — agentic-euchre, 2026-09-11 — carries the wrapped form and needs one run of the normaliser; noted on =6a207b00=, whose adoption work owns that step, rather than filed as its own heading.
:END:
:PLAN:
*What this actually poses is which parser owns* =:BLOCKER:=, *and the corpus is
larger than the filing counted.* Measured 2026-09-11 across both trackers: **36
blocker properties — 32 wrapped in =ids(...)=, 4 bare** (TODO.org 11 wrapped / 1
bare; DONE.org 21 / 3).

*Two coherent exits, and the data-side cost runs opposite to the intuition.*

- *Normalise to bare, keep org-depend.* Rewrite 32 properties plus the two
  =(format "ids(%s)" ...)= sites in =config.el=. No new dependency, and the read
  side already accepts both forms, so it is low-risk. It also keeps a parser
  whose own =Commentary:= header opens "WARNING: This file is just a PROOF OF
  CONCEPT, not a supported part of Org-mode", in a repository whose README says
  its files "receive little if no maintainance".
- *Enable =org-edna-mode=, convert the 4 bare ones.* **Smaller** on the data
  side — 4 properties against 32 — and it makes the syntax this repo has been
  writing all along *true* rather than accidental. One mandatory gate: under
  edna an unrecognised form is a **refusal**, so a bare uuid blocker would block
  unconditionally and never unblock. All 4 must be converted *before* enabling,
  not after.

*The architectural objection is narrower than it first reads, and the
distinction is the whole decision.* Adopting edna for its **blocker/finder
parsing alone** writes nothing, fires nothing and bypasses nothing. The queue
conflict — a trigger writing through Emacs during the human apply pass — arises
only from TRIGGER *actions*, which nothing here needs or would have to declare.
"Edna conflicts with the queue" is true of its triggers, not of its parser.

*What decides this is not in this body.* Every org-edna claim here comes from
reading its source; it is not installed, and nothing above has been run. This
repo's own rule is that a proposed mechanism is a hypothesis until measured, so
the next step is a batch trial against these real blocker shapes — both forms,
both parsers, a DONE blocker and an open one — not a decision. The cheap half of
the fix (normalising to bare) is available immediately and does not wait on that
trial.

*Fixing this turns on a second mechanism that is already enabled and currently
inert — decide it deliberately rather than discovering it.*
=org-depend-tag-blocked= defaults to =t=, nothing here or in the Doom config
sets it, and the live value is =t=. Its write is =(org-toggle-tag "blocked" (if
proceed-p 'off 'on))=: tag on a refusal, untag on a success. Because the
=ids(...)= form never parses, =proceed-p= is always non-nil today, so the tag is
only ever *removed* — zero instances in TODO.org, two historical ones in
DONE.org. **The moment blockers parse, every refused transition starts writing
=:blocked:= into the tracker.**

*Whether that is wanted is a real question with a filed precedent against it.*
[[id:a7f4b40c-d6e0-4bde-82ba-94bcf45253f4][a7f4b40c]] named the hazard as "a
derived fact stored is one that can go stale", and the staleness is concrete
here: the tag clears only when someone *re-attempts* the transition, so a
heading whose blocker is satisfied keeps its tag until something tries again.
Under the queue that is worse than under plain org, because attempts do not
happen when work finishes — they happen in bursts when a human runs the apply
pass.

*It is also not the inverse of =:BLOCKER:=, which is worth stating because the
name suggests it is.* =:BLOCKER:= *declares* a dependency on the blocked
heading; =:blocked:= *caches the verdict* of evaluating that same declaration.
The genuine other side — what a heading blocks — is stored nowhere and is
derivable by search; org-depend's =:TRIGGER:= is the closest thing and acts
forward rather than describing.

*So: settle the tag in the same change as the syntax.* Either set
=org-depend-tag-blocked= to nil and keep the record purely declarative, or keep
the tag and accept a derived value in the file — in which case the apply pass is
the natural place to refresh it, since that is the only moment transitions are
attempted.
:END:

=org-depend-block-todo= — the function actually on =org-blocker-hook= — does
=(split-string blocker)= and treats each whitespace-separated word as an exact
id. Wrapped in =ids(...)=, the first token arrives as =ids(<uuid>= and the last
as =<uuid>)=, and neither resolves; the =cond= falls through and nothing blocks.
The wrapper is org-edna's =ids()= *finder*, not org-depend's grammar, and
=config.el= writes it at two sites (the =org_set_property= validator and the
slice blocker refresh) under docstrings calling it "the documented =ids(a b c)=
form". Verified in batch against org 9.8.7 and the real =org-depend.el=, with
the bare form as a discriminating control: =ids(A)= → DONE, =ids(A B)= → DONE,
=ids(A B C)= → blocked but only by B, bare =A= → blocked. Measured over
TODO.org's 12 =:BLOCKER:= properties: 6 hold one id and 2 hold two, so **8
enforce nothing at all**; 3 hold 4, 9 and 14 ids and enforce all but their
endpoints; 1 is bare and fully enforced. Worst part is that it is invisible from
the read side — =--outline-blocker-ids= extracts ids by UUID regexp and finds
them all, so =org_outline='s =[blocked: ...]= marker is right while the
DONE-time refusal is not, and the divergence bites exactly at the human apply
pass where the refusal was supposed to appear. Two fixes: write a bare
space-separated list, or enable =org-edna-mode=, which parses =ids(...)=
natively. The second has a trap worth stating before anyone reaches for it —
with =org-edna-mode= on, a =:BLOCKER:= holding *bare* uuids raises an
unrecognised-form error, and org-edna's handler treats every parse failure as a
refusal, so those headings would block unconditionally. Found 2026-09-11 by a
research subagent surveying org's dependency facilities, and re-verified here
from scratch.

*Where the wrapper came from, read out of org-depend's own commentary 2026-09-11
— an honest misreading of a real API, not a typo.* org-depend **does** use
parentheses, on the =TRIGGER= side: =chain-siblings(KEYWORD)=,
=chain-find-next(KEYWORD[,OPTIONS])=, and =XYZ(KEYWORD)= where =XYZ= is an entry
id. Anyone reading that would reasonably infer a parallel parenthesised form for
=BLOCKER=. There is none. The blocker grammar is two rules and no more: the
literal word =previous-sibling=, or — "if the BLOCKER property contains any
other words, these are treated as entry id's… search for an entry with the ID
property **exactly equal to this word**". That "exactly equal" is what
=ids(<uuid>= fails.

*So the target form is simply* =:BLOCKER: uuid uuid uuid= — bare,
space-separated, no wrapper — which is also what the =org_set_property=
validator already produces internally before it wraps.

*Confirmed live rather than inferred:* =(featurep 'org-depend)= is =t=,
=(require 'org-depend)= sits in =~/.config/doom/config.el=, and
=org-depend-block-todo= is second of four on =org-blocker-hook= in the running
Emacs. So the parser that cannot read this syntax is the one actually guarding
every =DONE=.

*Resolved 2026-09-15* ([[orgit-rev:claude-code-ide-org::5f7ecdb][5f7ecdb]], corpus [[orgit-rev:claude-code-ide-org::b41897d][b41897d]], first member of =35582d95=). Both write sites now emit the bare space-separated form. A test drives =org-depend-block-todo= itself — three blockers written by the tool, the middle one finished first, DONE refused, then permitted once the endpoints finish — and against HEAD's write site it fails at exactly the "still TODO" assertion, so it discriminates. The read/enforce divergence is closed by a lint rule rather than by making the readers strict: they still find ids under either form, so an un-normalised consumer corpus still indexes, and =bin/lint-org= reports the wrapper as an error naming =claude-code-ide-org-normalize-blocker-syntax=, the idempotent rewrite with a dry run. Run through the live Emacs after a reload: 39 properties scanned, 35 unwrapped, a diff of exactly 35 lines, lint from 35 errors to 0. =org-edna-mode= declined for the reason above.



* TODO Nothing can correct a closed slice's record, even when the correction is close-time truth :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       a508c59e-475e-4d27-84c9-6c3ded98e15c
:CREATED:  [2026-09-11 Fri 17:17]
:CATEGORY: Slices
:END:

Second time the closed-slice guard has blocked a legitimate act.
=org_slice_add_member= refuses a closed slice and =refresh-slice= skips one,
both correctly — but on 2026-09-11 =c19fbbf5= needed two checkboxes ticked, a
keyword text corrected and a stale =:BLOCKER:= removed, all of which were *true
at its close* rather than later change. With no sanctioned mechanism the fix
went through the Edit tool, straight to disk behind Emacs's back, which is
exactly the buffer/disk divergence the queue architecture exists to prevent and
required a manual revert. Proposed: a narrow repair path — a refresh variant
that recomputes a closed slice against its referents' keywords *as of the
slice's CLOSED stamp*, or an explicit correct-closed-slice tool that writes
through Emacs. The distinction to preserve is the one the guard exists for:
re-deriving a closed slice against referents' keywords *today* is the
falsification =30a340fd= refused; deriving against their state at close is not.
Complements =a00ace04=, which is the detection half.

:PROPERTIES:

Found 2026-09-11 when the user spotted =c19fbbf5= sitting at =[17/19]=
with two members that had been =DONE= since before it closed. Nothing
mechanical could have caught it: the existing rule compares the slice's
=:BLOCKER:= against its *checklist*, and =--slice-blocker-ids= derives
that set from the checkbox cookies — so both halves read the same stale
boxes, agreed, and reported zero errors. The lint can detect a blocker
disagreeing with the boxes; it cannot detect boxes disagreeing with their
referents, which is the failure that actually occurs.

Proposed: a rule comparing each member's checkbox against the referent's current
keyword, per the conventions' derivation table. It must scope to *open* slices
only — on a closed one a disagreement is the record correctly refusing to track
later change (=30a340fd=), which is the opposite of a defect. Complements
=a508c59e=, which is the repair half; this one is detection.* TODO org_query
exposes four of org-ql's thirty-five predicates, so structured reads fall back
to text scanning :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       61f05e56-9d05-4f3e-9131-5eb899f95b8f
:CREATED:  [2026-09-11 Fri 16:16]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:

The user asked on 2026-09-11 why a session reaches for =sed=/=awk= against
TODO.org when =emacs-tools= exists; the answer is that the two questions at hand
were not expressible. =org_query= advertises =todo:=, =tags:=, =priority:= and
=heading:=, while the loaded org-ql defines 35 predicates (measured, not
recalled: =(length org-ql-predicates)=) including =property=, =closed=, =ts=,
=category=, =level=, =blocked=, =children=, =descendants=, =ancestors= and
=rifle=. So "which headings carry =:KIND: slice=" and "what closed after this
timestamp" both became hand-rolled awk — and the first attempt silently
mis-paired ids with headings, because =CLOSED:= precedes the property drawer and
the script did not model that. The engine already supports what is missing; this
is about the exposed surface. =property=, =closed= and =category= look
highest-value here, =blocked= notable since this project has bespoke code for
it.


* TODO apply-detect reports an apply when the review pass only dismissed events :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       7b4f4f14-6c76-4822-84c1-67589bc74b82
:CREATED:  [2026-09-11 Fri 15:45]
:CATEGORY: Apply
:END:

Found by PR22's review pass. =--queue-watermark-write= is the single writer for
=<session>.applied= and is called from both =--queue-mark-applied= and the
dismissal path, and it rewrites the file (atomic rename, new mtime) even when
nothing new landed. So a pass where a human only dismisses stale events makes
the next prompt carry "the review queue was applied since this session's last
turn … keywords and clocks reflect the apply" when nothing on disk changed — the
inverse of the freshness the hook exists to assert, and a claim a session will
relay. Either the watermark must distinguish applied-something from pass-ran, or
the hook must compare something other than its mtime. Introduced with
[[id:165ce65a-f87e-431c-8ee4-f0fbfffe14da][165ce65a]].


* TODO Every standalone client shares one project context, chosen by unstable ordering :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       965f94eb-1558-4d00-9857-5727eb86fe6f
:CREATED:  [2026-09-11 Fri 15:45]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:

Found by PR22's review pass. The shipped =.mcp.json= pins =/mcp/warp=, so every
standalone session — Warp, or =claude= in any terminal in any repo — resolves to
the session id =warp=, registered against =(car projects)=; under the glue's
='derive= setting that car is whatever =org-agenda-files= happens to list first.
A =claude= session started in repo B therefore captures into repo A's TODO.org,
and reordering =org-agenda-files= silently re-points it. Secondary defect in the
same place: using the directory basename as the session id means two tracked
repos sharing a basename collide, the later registration overwriting the
earlier. Sibling of [[id:d718f6b6-5a33-4889-8b5a-8ea82553ccea][d718f6b6]].

Found by PR22's review pass. =--review-apply-capture= resolves a nil target
through =--capture-target-file=, which consults the MCP session context — but
upstream =let=-binds =--current-session-id= around the HTTP request only, so
during =M-x claude-code-ide-org-review= it is nil and the heading falls back to
the global capture file. So: capture with no target from project B while B's
TODO.org has unsaved edits, the call defers and answers "top of TODO.org", a
human applies, and the heading lands in project A's tracker. This is
[[id:d718f6b6-5a33-4889-8b5a-8ea82553ccea][d718f6b6]] surviving on its queued
half. The queue event already carries =cwd= (=bin/hooks/queue-append:229=) and
nothing on the apply path reads it, which is the likely fix.

Asked by the user 2026-09-11. The signal already exists: apply writes .applied
watermark files in ~/.claude/org-updates/, so no elisp changes. Build a
UserPromptSubmit hook (bin/hooks/apply-detect, plain-shell tier) that compares
the watermarks' state against a per-session last-seen stamp and injects
additionalContext naming how many items landed since the session's last turn —
the session then commits the tracker diff unprompted, and the user's next
message needs no announcement. A per-session background Monitor watching the
same files is the opt-in fully-hands-free variant (wakes the session to commit
while the user stays in Emacs); the hook is the standing mechanism because it
rides natural turn boundaries instead of interrupting. Wire in both
.claude/settings.json and the plugin's hooks/hooks.json, one or the other per
repo as ever.

Built same day: =bin/hooks/apply-detect= on =UserPromptSubmit=, comparing the
apply pass's =.applied= watermarks against a per-session =.apply-seen= stamp and
injecting =additionalContext= once per apply — silent on quiet turns, silent on
a session's first prompt (that history is SessionStart's report), exit 0 on
every failure path so it can never block a prompt. Wired in both surfaces,
documented as the hooks table's one read-only row, 13-case suite green. REVIEW
exit: hook config is read at session start, so the proof is the *next* session
reacting to an apply nobody announced.

*Partial proof 2026-09-11, and deliberately not closed on it.* The hook
fired in a later session than the one that built it, reported =3
watermark(s) moved= against a real apply, and that session committed the
tracker diff (=765192e=) without being told to — the mechanism works end
to end, counts included.

What it does *not* prove is this heading's own exit test, "the next
session reacting to an apply nobody announced": the user's message that
turn opened with "applied.", so the announcement came anyway and the hook
and the human arrived together. Whether the hook *removes* the
announcement is therefore still unobserved, which is the behavioural
claim the whole heading is about. Left in =REVIEW= for the next session
that applies and says nothing; that costs nothing and the test is
unambiguous when it happens. Note the earlier REVIEW note already
anticipated this precisely — "the next session's unprompted reaction is
the exit" — so accepting today's run would be weakening a criterion after
seeing the result.

*Shipped:* the prompt join and tracker naming in the review buffer,
newest-first ordering with =s=, six faces, the readable session render,
=M-x claude-code-ide-org-attention-report=, =M-x
claude-code-ide-org-allocation-report=, =M-x

(=b23e5bcc=, =169df26b=). 654 ERT tests at close against 601 at
composition.

*This is the first slice to carry its own measurements, and the numbers
are the baseline =next-session.md= asks the next one to beat.*

| members | 11 (9 planned, 2 adopted) |
| span | 2026-09-11 composed, 2026-09-15 merged — 5 calendar days, 3 working |
| commits | 50 |
| review findings | 7, i.e. 0.64 per member |
| attention | **8.0 h** on the slice plus 2.2 h meta-work over the window |

*Note what the attention figure is and is not.* 8.0 h is the human's
*attention*, derived by this slice's own tooling — not agent run time,
which is roughly a third of it. A slice measuring itself with the
instrument it built is a pleasing result and a circular one: the number
is only as good as the derivation, which nothing has yet reconciled
against a calendar.

*What went wrong, because it is the more useful half.* Three defects
were introduced *and* survived a green suite: the fill sweep wrapped
slice member lines and a later refresh orphaned their tails as duplicated
prose; =fill-prose= reverted the buffer on its *dry-run* path, which was
the interactive default, so its safest-looking invocation could destroy
unsaved work; and a 604 KB =config.elc= was committed four times, able to
shadow source edits silently. None was caught by =bin/test=, and two were
caught only by an external review. That is =8a2eb687='s thesis
demonstrated three times inside one slice, by the slice's own author.

*Falsified along the way*, each recorded on its member: that a slice
rollup would assign time predating the slice (0.8 h of 10.0 h, not the
60% first measured); that the queue could say which headings a window
worked (it records whether the human had the file open); and that
=b23e5bcc= was blocked on =169df26b= (reading the call chain answered it).

*Left behind, and filed:* =7ee3b71a= (=org_amend replace== erases a
slice's checklist), now a member of =8a2eb687=; and =369ad62a= (a
bookkeeping burst inflates a window's sharing count). Nothing else is
outstanding — every review finding was fixed on the branch and every
thread resolved before the merge.
:END:

:PLAN:
The foundation is one measured fact (=295cde3a=, 2026-09-01): every queue
=resume= event matches a transcript user message on timestamp — 28 of 28 within
two seconds — so the prompts are already durably recorded beside a key that
joins them to the record exactly, retroactively, across every session ever kept.
The standing trap travels with the join: *the queue defines what a prompt is* —
raw =type:"user"= transcript entries are contaminated by stop-hook feedback and
tool results (=9e627dc0=, 45 raw against 36 real).

Three deliverables, sequenced smallest first:

1. *=325679af= — the review-buffer surfacing.* An item's synopsis today is its
   enclosing clock event's note (=--review-annotation-label=), so
   guidepost-reconstructed spans have none by construction — the user cannot
   discriminate a degenerate 0-run span from an unmeasured long turn. Render
   each run's opening prompt, and for a lone-timestamp span the abutting prompts
   either side, truncated. No hook change, no new durable field.

2. *=96ddf1ef= — the readable session.* Render a transcript to org for paging in
   Emacs: isearch, folding, no scroll position lost to the agent answering. The
   walk-the-whole-session tool where the per-span excerpt is not enough;
   promoted from MAYBE the day the review-context need became concrete.

3. *=295cde3a= — weighted attribution, research.* The same join read for
   meaning: 84% of turns name at least one known heading id (mean 6.2), so a
   turn can be attributed to several tasks by weight, with lag. Builds on 1 and
   2; explicitly research, expected to end in a design rather than necessarily
   code.

The sequencing is by dependency of *confidence*, not code: each deliverable
trusts the join a little further than the one before.

*Amended at declaration's end, same day: =fbaf8009= joins as deliverable 0 and
executes FIRST*, though the checklist shows it last — =org_slice_add_member= has
no prepend (filed as its own gap), and re-declaring the slice to fix cosmetic
order is not worth it. It is the cheapest of the four and needs no transcript
join at all: the =cwd= is already on every queue event (=5461c349=) and the
destination tracker falls out of where the id resolves — pure rendering. The
review pass gets *where* before it gets *what*.
:END:

The record knows *when* work happened but not *what was going on* — a 0-run span
at review time is indistinguishable from an unmeasured long turn, and the user
can never recall the interval. The transcripts already hold the answer, joined
to the queue by timestamp (28 of 28 within two seconds). This slice spends that
join three ways, smallest first: prompt excerpts beside every span in the review
buffer, whole sessions rendered to readable org, and turn-to-task attribution by
weight. Declared 2026-09-11 at the user's motion, the day the review-context
need became concrete.

Planned:

- [X] [[id:325679af-829e-4c7f-88b0-153c2c9683b3][325679af]] DONE The review buffer shows each run's opening prompt, joined from the transcript
- [X] [[id:96ddf1ef-67fd-4d3c-b8cc-01ebb14a687d][96ddf1ef]] DONE Render a session transcript to readable org, so scrollback stops being the index
- [X] [[id:295cde3a-49a5-4960-a828-a4fc175d73c1][295cde3a]] DONE Divide a window's run time among the headings the transcript shows it worked
- [X] [[id:fbaf8009-b3dc-48ed-b099-63b74fc156a8][fbaf8009]] DONE The review buffer should name each item's tracker, now that two projects share the queue
- [X] [[id:f1e36a65-3469-4ae6-ac1f-fdfcd1c053c9][f1e36a65]] DONE Open the review buffer newest-first, with =s= to reverse it like dired
- [X] [[id:f3a53bd4-60ab-47a1-84b0-07d2c1c5fd24][f3a53bd4]] DONE The review buffer is one undifferentiated face, so its element kinds have to be parsed by eye
- [X] [[id:40159d43-e48b-4f38-95eb-651145637049][40159d43]] DONE Nothing stops a span being assigned to "Review attention", which ranks high and reads like "Review and planning"
- [X] [[id:b23e5bcc-1bfd-4862-a801-0c7ac6959924][b23e5bcc]] DONE A state item applies correctly and is then reported failed, so it is re-offered forever
- [X] [[id:169df26b-64a8-4de9-8919-ab58d510aaf6][169df26b]] DONE The stored error backtrace is captured after unwinding, so it never shows where the error came from
- [X] [[id:4f8500e6-b468-4fd4-a0f6-ebf04c7a57ba][4f8500e6]] DONE Reconstruct coarse day-level attention intervals for the agenda
- [X] [[id:496b665e-3370-4a66-814a-3c5bd2d3dddc][496b665e]] DONE Long prose lines accumulate in the trackers, and M-q by hand is the only thing wrapping them

*Adopted 2026-09-11 under the new discovery rule:* =f1e36a65= (open the review
buffer newest-first, =s= to reverse) surfaced while this slice's first two
members were being built — same buffer, unclaimed, cheap, and wanted — so it
joins as an unplanned member rather than waiting to be listed as incidental
after it closes. The cookie moves from =[0/4]= to =[0/5]=, which is the
denominator-before-numerator regression the convention now warns about, not a
loss of progress.

*Four other discoveries from the same day were **not** adopted, and the reasons
are the two the rule allows.* =c9940558=, =b57c7515= and =2deb090f= are
**already members of [[id:749301a0-0bd0-4fbd-b3f7-79739226e15a][749301a0]]**,
and a member counted by two slices makes their cookies disagree about the same
work — so they are cross-referenced here rather than adopted. =3f4fd744= (the
=:BLOCKER:= parser) has no relationship to the transcript join at all; it was
found by a delegated survey on an unrelated question and stands alone.

*One of those cross-references is worth reading before Step 3.* =b57c7515=
established that a session id can change mid-work, leaving **two transcripts for
one conversation** — the earlier truncated at the switch, the later complete
from the original start. =96ddf1ef= renders a session transcript to org and
would, keyed naively on session id, render that conversation twice with the
first copy silently short. That is a design input, not a defect to fix here.

*Adopted 2026-09-14, retroactively and at the user's prompting* — they asked
when incidentals would appear, and the answer exposed that they should not have
been waiting for incidentals at all. An incidental is derived from a **close**,
and of thirteen headings filed while this slice ran, twelve are =TODO= and one
=DOING=; nothing unplanned has closed, so the =Incidental:= section was
correctly empty and would have stayed so indefinitely. Under the adoption rule
the user set the same morning
([[id:9bab114d-44fa-488e-92bd-ff7c3fd8065d][9bab114d]]) these belonged here *at
discovery*, which is exactly what "see them accumulate along the way" asks for.
Four joined: =f3a53bd4= (the review buffer's faces), =40159d43= (=Review
attention= is assignable at =a= and ranks well), =b23e5bcc= (a landed state
change reported failed) and =169df26b= (the backtrace captured after unwinding,
which blocks =b23e5bcc='s diagnosis). Cookie =[4/5]= → =[4/8]=: the
denominator-before-numerator regression the convention warns about, not lost
progress.

*Three were not adopted because another slice already counts them* — =c9940558=,
=b57c7515= and =2deb090f= are members of
[[id:749301a0-0bd0-4fbd-b3f7-79739226e15a][749301a0]], and a member counted
twice makes two cookies disagree. Cross-referenced here instead, per the rule.
=3f4fd744=, =b0d55552=, =b52df20b=, =6a207b00= and =9bab114d= itself have no
relationship to this slice's surface.

*And the depth-one rule met its first genuine ambiguity, which is the trial's
first real datum.* =e95cc5d1= and =ee3c22c9= — both about what =d= should do —
were discovered while the user dismissed the stale items that =b23e5bcc=
produced. If =b23e5bcc= is an adopted member, they are discoveries *from an
adopted member* and should seed the next slice; if the dismissing is read as
ordinary review-pass work, they are depth one and belong here. **The distinction
was not readable in the moment**, which is precisely what the trial was set up
to find out. Left unadopted pending the user's ruling rather than decided
quietly, because deciding it silently would waste the trial.

*Correction 2026-09-14, and it applies to the checklist above rather than to any
member.* **Five of the nine members listed under =Planned:= were never
planned.** Measured against the operative =next-session.md=: =325679af=,
=96ddf1ef=, =295cde3a= and =fbaf8009= appear there two or three times each;
=f1e36a65=, =f3a53bd4=, =40159d43=, =b23e5bcc= and =169df26b= appear **zero**
times. The four were adopted earlier today and =f1e36a65= this morning, all
through =org_slice_add_member=, which writes to the planned checklist because
that is the only list it can write to.

*The lead is therefore currently false for five lines*, and that is recorded
here rather than quietly tolerated: the =Planned:= marker means what it says,
and the user's test — absence from the plan file — is what gives it content
([[id:9bab114d-44fa-488e-92bd-ff7c3fd8065d][9bab114d]]). They are left in place
rather than reverted because removing them would restore the invisibility that
prompted adopting them; the fix is a writable =Incidental:= section, not a
shorter list.

*Read the cookie accordingly.* =[4/9]= counts four planned members done out of
four planned plus five adopted. The slice's own deliverable — the transcript
join, its three ways — is 4 of 5 with =295cde3a= outstanding; the other five are
work this slice caused or uncovered, not work it set out to do.

*Plan revisions* (added 2026-09-14, late — the convention wants this from the
moment a slice is picked up, and this slice ran three days without it):

- [[orgit-rev:claude-code-ide-org::add7291][add7291]] [2026-09-11 Fri 11:13]
  rewrote =next-session.md= for this slice: nominated it and named =fbaf8009= as
  its opening member

*This link is what makes the planned/adopted distinction derivable on demand*,
which is the reason the project declined to store that distinction in the file
([[id:9bab114d-44fa-488e-92bd-ff7c3fd8065d][9bab114d]]). =next-session.md= is
rewritten in place, so the *current* file answers the question only until the
next slice rewrites it; after that the pinned revision is the only way to ask
what this slice actually planned. Without the link the grep degrades into
guessing a commit by date, which is precisely the kind of
recoverable-in-principle that stops being done.

*Integration point:*
[[https://github.com/nbest937/claude-code-ide-org.el/pull/23][PR #23]], opened
2026-09-14 with 43 commits. Under the close condition settled on =e80f6ccf= this
slice closes when that merges, not when its last member went terminal — which is
why it sits at =REVIEW= now rather than =DONE=.

*Resolution:* all eleven members shipped and
[[https://github.com/nbest937/claude-code-ide-org.el/pull/23][PR #23]]
merged 2026-09-15 as =3b1b14c=, which is this slice's close condition
rather than its last member going terminal. The review buffer now
answers *where* and *what* for every span, a session renders to readable
