The analogue of =35d25265= one drawer over. After that pass extracted
the plans, each finished heading's remaining body *is* its debrief —
so the wrap itself needs no seam judgement, exactly as =d5eb32a3='s
ordering finding predicted. What each heading still needs is an
**authored resolution line**: one-to-two sentences left in the body
before the debrief moves into =:DEBRIEF:=. Measured 2026-09-02, 88 of
93 unwrapped headings carry a debrief, so this is ~88 short summaries
— per-heading judgement, human in the loop or at minimum
human-reviewed in batches, never a blind sweep. Mechanically: for each
finished heading with prose below its drawers, author the resolution,
then move the remainder into =:DEBRIEF:= (=org_amend drawer=DEBRIEF=
serves the write; whether a dedicated seam-free wrapper is worth
building can be decided when the pass starts). This is the
precondition =2a399034= names: until bodies are short corpus-wide,
carrying them in the outline makes it more expensive, not less.

Cross-note 2026-09-09: =2a399034= shipped the read split *before* this
pass, at the user's call — so when this heading closes, tell the user
it is time to re-measure the unscoped =org_outline= (bodies now ride
in the index) and retire the interim =bodies=false= advice in
CLAUDE.md's "Reading the tracker" if the numbers support it. The full
reminder lives in =2a399034='s =:DEBRIEF:=.

*The org conventions were corrected against this heading 2026-09-15,
having been stricter than their own source.* They summarised the
paragraph above as "a per-heading act nobody should do by sweep", where
this body says "human in the loop **or at minimum human-reviewed in
batches**, never a *blind* sweep" — a prohibition where the source
permits a reviewed pass. Caught when the user gave full latitude on this
work and asked why that would be wrong; the honest answer was that the
rule being quoted at them was not the rule. *That is the failure CLAUDE.md
names in its opening section — a claim that loads every session
outranking the artifact it describes — arriving in the conventions rather
than in CLAUDE.md.*

*And the correction added the shape this pass should take*, which was
nowhere written: **three passes, not one.** Relocation is lossless and
verifiable by a non-whitespace digest. Authoring a resolution is additive
and *unverifiable* — =bin/lint-org= can assert one is present, never that
it is true, and a folded heading shows the resolution *instead of* the
body, so a wrong one displaces the record rather than sitting beside it.
Condensation is the pare no later reader catches. So: relocate first and
wholesale, author in reviewable batches, condense last and separately.

*Checked at the same time and needing nothing:* whether
[[id:d350ff5b-3b1f-465e-800b-962264d2e841][d350ff5b]] must precede this. It need
not. =org_amend drawer=DEBRIEF= already writes the drawer in canonical position
— verified on =496b665e=, whose order is =:PROPERTIES:=, =:LOGBOOK:=, =:PLAN:=,
=:DEBRIEF:=, then body — so this pass creates no disorder for that one to clean
up, and the two are independent in either order. The slice lists =d350ff5b=
earlier, which is sequence rather than dependency; no =:BLOCKER:= is warranted.


* TODO A boolean argument passed as false arrives as :false, which elisp reads as true :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       a8ccd6ac-3452-4926-b176-ff5951dcd5b2
:CREATED:  [2026-09-02 Wed 12:50]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:

*Found 2026-09-02 while fixing
[[id:72463b68-9f08-44b2-8213-87f71298138d][72463b68]], noticed in passing and
measured rather than left as a suspicion.*

The MCP server parses a request body with =(json-parse-string body :object-type
'alist)=, whose default =:false-object= is the keyword =:false=. So a JSON
=false= arrives in elisp as the symbol =:false=, and *every non-nil value is
true in elisp*. Measured:

: (json-parse-string "{\"append\":false}" :object-type 'alist)
: => ((append . :false))       ; and (if :false ...) takes the true branch

**The caller gets the opposite of what they asked for**, and only when they are
explicit. Omitting the argument yields =nil= and behaves correctly, so the bug
is invisible to every call that leaves the default alone — which is why it has
never been seen.

Two live arguments, and the second is destructive:

- =org_set_property='s =append=. Passing =false= unions with the existing
  =:BLOCKER:= set instead of replacing it. Wrong, recoverable.
- =org_amend='s =replace=. Passing =false= **replaces the heading's entire
  prose** instead of appending to it. That tool's own description says
  "Destructive and irreversible except through git", and this reaches it by
  asking for the *safe* behaviour.

*Not hypothetical about which tools are exposed:* those are the only two boolean
=:type=s in the surface today, and both were touched on 2026-09-02.

*The fix is one binding, not a per-tool audit.* Parsing with =:false-object nil=
makes JSON =false= arrive as =nil= and read correctly everywhere at once. The
alternative — teaching each handler to recognise =:false= — is the shape this
repo keeps rejecting: a rule applied per site that a later site will forget.
Note the change belongs *upstream* in =claude-code-ide-mcp-http-server.el=
beside [[id:ce012ad4-87f7-435c-a2ee-25210c071865][ce012ad4]], since the parse is
theirs; a local override would have to shadow their function.

*Same family as [[id:6495c8a0-d971-4c77-8b30-9fde1bbf5f26][6495c8a0]]* — a tool
accepting an argument and not honouring it — but with the failure inverted.
There the value is silently discarded; here it is silently *negated*, which is
worse, because discarding leaves the default in place while negating actively
performs the operation the caller declined.


* TODO claude-code-ide-org-capture reports a queued capture it never persists :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       8326a46f-e3f9-4ecc-8bc2-05d54907987b
:CREATED:  [2026-09-02 Wed 12:09]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:

*Split out of [[id:72463b68-9f08-44b2-8213-87f71298138d][72463b68]] on
2026-09-02*, which called this "the dangerous one" and "the last matters most".
Its body holds the original observation; only the residue is restated here.

Called directly over =emacsclient=, outside the MCP wrapper,
=claude-code-ide-org-capture= returned ="Queued capture: … (ID: …) pending
review"= and wrote *nothing* — not to the session queue file, whose mtime never
moved, not elsewhere under =~/.claude/org-updates/=, and not to any org file.
The ids it reported existed nowhere.

*The presumed mechanism, still unverified:* the queue path needs a session
context the MCP wrapper supplies and a bare =emacsclient= call lacks. If that is
right the failure is silent by construction — the caller is told the work is
recorded, which is the worst available outcome and strictly worse than an error.

*The fix is a refusal, not a repair.* A capture that cannot determine where to
queue must say so loudly rather than report success. Whether the =emacsclient=
path should additionally be *made* to queue is a separate and smaller question;
a tool that cannot persist must not claim it did, however it was invoked.

*Why this outlived its parent's fix.*
[[id:72463b68-9f08-44b2-8213-87f71298138d][72463b68]]'s hang is now diagnosed
and fixed — three tools declared a semantically optional argument required, and
the resulting =json-rpc-error= escaped every handler. That fix removes the
*reason* to reach for the =emacsclient= fallback, which is exactly why this one
must not be quietly dropped with it: the trap is now rarer and no less silent,
and rarity is what keeps a false success unnoticed.

:PROPERTIES:

*Split out of [[id:72463b68-9f08-44b2-8213-87f71298138d][72463b68]] on
2026-09-02*, which found and fixed the hang but left this untouched. It was the
second of that heading's three named fixes.

=(cl-defun claude-code-ide-org-capture (title &optional target tags note
initial-state))= names =note= in its argument list and references it nowhere in
the body — re-confirmed 2026-09-02, still true. The tool description promises
more than the arglist does: it says the value is "recorded on the queued event
when the write defers", so a caller who supplies one has been told it is kept.

This is [[id:1cd2df62-35e9-47dc-bcce-dda7bae3c522][1cd2df62]]'s "accepts
arguments it does not have, and says nothing" in this project's own
implementation, which is what makes it worth fixing rather than documenting
away.

*Two ways to close it, and the choice is the work.* Either record =note= on the
deferred-capture event as the description already claims, or drop the parameter
and the sentence together. What must not survive is the present state, where the
schema advertises a parameter that is silently discarded.

Note the near neighbour that is now *not* a defect: =org_capture= also once
dropped a *body*, and three headings arrived empty because of it. That is the
same shape but a different argument, and it is not what this heading is about.


* TODO claude-code-ide-org-review should return point and windows to where I entered from :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       6c64cc9c-33a9-4a20-af19-c188a690c755
:CREATED:  [2026-09-02 Wed 09:09]
:CATEGORY: Apply
:END:

*Asked by the user 2026-09-02:* =M-x claude-code-ide-org-review= should
return them to the point they were at when they entered it.

*Locatable, checked the same day.*
=claude-code-ide-org-review= ends in =(pop-to-buffer buffer)= and saves
no window configuration and no point marker, so nothing exists to
restore from. Two things then move the user:

- =pop-to-buffer= itself rearranges windows, and burying the review
  buffer afterwards does not put back what was displaced.
- =claude-code-ide-org-review-goto= jumps to a heading in the org file,
  so point in TODO.org is left wherever the last =RET= landed rather
  than where the human was reading.

*Note the exit is not only =q=.* Burying the review buffer is what runs
the ceremony — drawer consolidation, heading separation, archiving,
sorting — so the restore has to happen *after* four steps that write to
the very buffer the saved point lives in. A raw marker into TODO.org can
therefore be invalidated by the ceremony's own edits: archiving removes
subtrees and sorting reorders every top-level heading, which is
precisely the case a naive =point-marker= gets wrong.

*So the interesting decision is what "where I was" means* once the
ceremony has rewritten the file underneath it. Candidates, none chosen:

- *Window configuration only* — =current-window-configuration= before
  =pop-to-buffer=, restored on quit. Cheap and correct for the layout
  half; says nothing about point moving inside TODO.org.
- *Restore by =:ID:= rather than by position* — record the heading point
  was inside on entry and navigate back to it by id. Survives sorting
  and archiving by construction, which a marker does not. Degrades
  honestly when the heading was archived: it moved to DONE.org and can
  be followed there, or the restore can decline and say so.
- *Both,* which is probably the answer: layout from the window
  configuration, position from the id.

*Adjacent, and worth not conflating:*
[[id:29734f79-e698-43dd-abf8-14bc86b8b0ae][29734f79]] made the ceremony
report its own completion on bury. This heading is about what the human
sees *after* that report — the same moment, a different property of it.

*Done when* entering and leaving the review command is position-neutral:
same window layout, and point in TODO.org where it started, or an
explicit message saying why it could not be.


* TODO Classify gaps by markers at either end, not by tracking an AFK state :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       409a69ff-d57e-4c1c-ab6c-28f7e85fa391
:CREATED:  [2026-09-01 Tue 14:33]
:CATEGORY: Queue
:END:

Split out of [[id:e75d660f-5c0d-4707-a645-b5dea7d7ebc0][e75d660f]] 2026-09-01 at
the user's request, because it is a mechanism rather than the question of
whether markers are worth capturing at all.

*The refinement: classify each **gap**, using markers at either end, instead of
tracking an AFK **state** forward from departure markers.*

*Why the state model fails, and it failed twice before this was found.* An
opener can be ambiguous. "afk since 13:30" reads equally as "I was away and am
back" and as "I forgot to say AFK and still am" — the user made exactly that
objection, and the data agreed: that prompt was followed by a *77-minute* gap. A
state machine keyed on openers has to resolve that ambiguity to proceed, and
cannot.

*Classifying gaps sidesteps it entirely, because the closing prompt is also
evidence.* That 77-minute gap is labelled by its *closer* — an explicit "BAK" —
with no need to decide what its opener meant. A gap is an absence when its
opening prompt carries a departure marker *or* its closing prompt carries a
return marker. An ambiguous opener becomes harmless rather than blocking.

*A prompt is defined by the queue, not the transcript.* Stop-hook feedback is
injected into the transcript in the *user role*, so a naive scan counts it as a
prompt: 45 raw =type:"user"= entries against *36* real prompts this session, and
an earlier measurement here was computed over the contaminated set. The queue's
=resume= events fire only on =UserPromptSubmit=, so they are the definition; the
transcript supplies the text at those timestamps. This is
[[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]]'s recorded trap — /"276
=type:"user"= entries, almost all tool results"/ — met again from a different
direction.

*Marker detection is sentence-bounded, not substring.* =afk= must stand as its
own sentence, optionally preceded by =still=. A substring test matches any
conversation *about* the markers, which is the mention-versus-subject trap that
also governs the footnote modes
([[id:2a6a1355-615f-4c2c-a8af-c9ae8dcda09b][2a6a1355]]) and the
weighted-attribution candidate set
([[id:295cde3a-49a5-4960-a828-a4fc175d73c1][295cde3a]]) — three appearances in
one day, which is what makes it worth naming rather than fixing three times.

*Coverage is partial and that is the design, not a shortfall.* Of this session's
seven longest inter-prompt gaps, markers label three — one by its opener, one by
its closer, one by its opener — and four carry nothing, including one of 776
minutes. So markers *override* the threshold where present and the threshold
remains the fallback. Aiming for complete coverage would mean annotating every
gap, which is the clerical burden this line of work exists to remove.

*One consequence for whatever reports this:* a labelled gap and an inferred one
must not be presented as the same thing. The honest output says which gaps carry
human testimony and which rest on the 1200s heuristic, rather than one number
covering both.

*Deferred, and named so it is not mistaken for an oversight:* the *retrospective
correction* channel — a prompt that labels a gap already past, like "afk since
13:30" does. The gap rule above does not need it, but it is a richer signal than
a binary marker and would let a human correct the record in passing.

*The user characterised their own AFK discipline 2026-09-04, and it undercuts
the marker more than anything measured so far:* "interpretation of =AFK= relies
on my discipline, which I'm sure has been inconsistent. Often I will pop in,
make a quick judgment or queue a prompt during a long-running turn, and then pop
out again without mentioning 'still AFK'."

**That is fatal to the state-tracking reading and awkward for this heading's own
one.** Tracking an AFK *state* fails because nothing clears it. But classifying
by *markers at either end* assumes the markers bound the absence, and the
behaviour described puts a marker in the *middle* of one — an interjection
proving a moment of attention without ending the absence around it. So a gap
opened by =AFK= and closed by a substantive prompt may be absence, attention, or
absence-attention-absence, and nothing distinguishes the third.

*Observed the same day, in the session that recorded this.* The span
=[2026-09-03 Thu 15:05]--[15:17]= opens eleven minutes after an =AFK=
interjection and closes with a prompt arguing against a decision made in the
preceding turn — unwritable without having read it. Both halves are true of one
twelve-minute interval.

**The user's conclusion, and it is the right one: "formulating a deterministic
heuristic is a fool's errand."** This heading should not be read as promising
one. What survives is weaker and still worth having: markers *raise or lower
confidence* on a span a human is going to judge anyway, which is =5a6d2c75='s
frame rather than an automatic classifier.


* TODO AFK markers are ground truth for the one judgement we cannot automate, and we discard them :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       e75d660f-5c0d-4707-a645-b5dea7d7ebc0
:CREATED:  [2026-09-01 Tue 13:11]
:CATEGORY: Queue
:END:

*Asked by the user 2026-09-01: "how are we currently using my AFK/BAK markers?"
Nowhere mechanically. They exist only in the conversation.*

*Verified rather than recalled.* A =resume= event carries four fields — =ts=,
=kind=, =session_id=, =source=. Across *1868* =resume=/=pause= events in every
queue file including the archive, no other field is ever non-null.
=bin/hooks/session-resume= is one line, =exec queue-append resume=, and
=queue-append= nulls =id=, =state=, =from= and =note= for the session-global
kinds by design. *The prompt text is never captured.*

*What the markers have actually been used for, in-session only:* deciding
whether to keep working unattended; writing an AFK boundary into prose by hand —
[[id:c31b6c76-8ad8-45d4-a87b-39e085e31368][c31b6c76]]'s body records "the user
reports being AFK from 13:30" because a session put it there deliberately; and
reasoning about which spans not to trust. None of that reaches the queue, and
the review pass has no idea any of it was said.

*Which is the finding, and it is sharper than a missing feature.* The markers
are *precisely the ground truth for the one judgement the system cannot make*.
[[id:96a51c2f-ce11-4b40-bdff-11223d3d4eb7][96a51c2f]] measured that gap *length*
separates presence from absence while clock hour does not — 11 of 19 long gaps
began inside working hours — so the system has no way to tell an AFK gap from a
thinking gap. And [[id:20cecd53-2338-4237-ab06-f8129e10a2c8][20cecd53]] found
that the only judgement the human-to-agent ratio needs is "were you at the
keyboard", on five or six gaps a day.

*The user has been answering that question unprompted, in band, at the moment of
highest confidence — and it is thrown away every time.* On 2026-09-01 there were
5 gaps of 1200s or more and the boundaries of most were stated in the
conversation as they happened. That is a labelled dataset arriving free.

*Why this is worth more than the retrospective alternative.* Every other path to
the same label asks the human to reconstruct, hours later, whether they were
present during a gap they no longer remember. A marker typed at the boundary is
contemporaneous and costs nothing, because the user is already typing it for
their own reasons.

*Options, unranked and undecided:*

1. *Capture the whole prompt on =resume=.* Maximal information, and the wrong
   default: the queue is a durable, unencrypted log that outlives the session,
   and putting every prompt in it is a privacy and size decision far larger than
   this question.
2. *Capture a marker only* — detect an AFK/BAK-shaped token and record a
   boolean, or a distinct =kind=. Narrow, cheap, and leaves the queue's contents
   as bounded as they are today.
3. *Ask at review instead*, listing the long gaps and taking an answer per gap.
   No hook change, but it asks the human to remember rather than to have said.

*A precondition for any of them, unverified:* whether the =UserPromptSubmit=
payload reaching =queue-append= actually carries the prompt text. Everything
above assumes it does. That is one =jq= away and should be checked before any of
this is designed, not after.

*Related but distinct:* [[id:1a5a5254-f62d-46e7-9a6c-3d84f2f52873][1a5a5254]]
proposes reading power assertions as an attribution signal — machine evidence
for the same question. This is the human's own testimony, which is cheaper and
probably more accurate, and the two are not exclusive.

*Measured 2026-09-01, and it replaces all three options above with a fourth —
plus one problem that is harder than expected.*

*The precondition is moot, because the data is already joinable.* Claude Code's
own transcripts live in =~/.claude/projects/<slug>/<session>.jsonl= — 48 of them
for this project — and every user message carries an ISO timestamp. *Every one
of this session's 28 =resume= events matches a transcript user message within
two seconds; 28 of 28.* So the prompt text is already durably recorded beside a
key that joins it to the queue exactly.

*Option 4, which dominates 1-3: join queue to transcript on timestamp.* No hook
change, no new field in a durable log, no privacy decision about what the queue
should hold, and it works *retroactively* across every session ever recorded
rather than only forwards.

*The =UserPromptSubmit= payload question is therefore unanswered and no longer
blocking.* It was not checked: verifying it means teeing a live hook that runs
on every prompt, and the join removes the reason to. It matters only if a *live
in-session* marker is ever wanted, which nothing yet needs.

*The hard part is not access, it is the labelling rule — and two attempts at one
both failed.* A gap should be labelled absent when the message opening the
preceding turn says the user is leaving, or the message ending the gap says they
are back. Both drafts over-labelled: the first treated any earlier AFK message
as labelling every later gap, the second still marked gaps of *5 and 14 seconds*
as absences. Two causes, and both are structural rather than sloppy:

- *Mid-turn messages break the pairing.* The user can send "still AFK" while the
  agent is working, which writes a =resume= that does not correspond to the
  start of a wait. The pause/resume stream stops being an alternation.
- *"Back" is ambiguous in ordinary prose* — "go back to the slice" is not a
  return announcement — while "BAK" is unambiguous and rarer.

*So the honest state: the signal is real and the access is solved; the
extraction is not.* Rates from the flawed rules are not reported here on
purpose, since both are known wrong and a wrong number is worse than none
([[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]]).

*What would settle it is a handful of human judgements rather than a better
regex.* Twenty-odd long gaps in this project's history plausibly carry a marker;
the user can confirm or reject each in a few minutes, and that becomes the
labelled set a rule is *validated against* rather than derived from. That is
also the first ground truth the 1200s threshold has ever had, which is
[[id:96a51c2f-ce11-4b40-bdff-11223d3d4eb7][96a51c2f]]'s September occurrence.

*The imputation rule, proposed by the user 2026-09-01, fixes the part my two
attempts got wrong:* declare that *any prompt without "still AFK" or similar
means BAK*. The user noted they had probably forgotten a BAK this session, and
that a mechanical consumer should not depend on remembering one.

*This makes it a state machine rather than a sticky flag, which is precisely
where both earlier drafts failed.* Absence *ends* at the next unmarked prompt
instead of persisting until an explicit return. Presence becomes the default and
absence the marked state — so a forgotten BAK costs nothing, and the "any
earlier AFK message labels every later gap" failure cannot occur by
construction.

*Detecting the marker is the remaining problem, and it is the
mention-versus-subject trap for the third time today* — after the footnote modes
and the weighted-attribution candidate set. A substring test for =afk= matches
this very conversation, which is *about* AFK markers.

*Sentence-boundedness resolves it, measured on this session's 45 prompts:*
require =afk= (optionally preceded by =still=) to stand as its own sentence —
delimiter or string start before, delimiter or end after. Eight prompts mention
=afk=; the rule marks *five* and rejects three, and all three rejections are
correct:

| prompt | verdict |
|--------+---------|
| "how are we currently using my AFK/BAK markers?" | discussion, correctly ignored |
| "re: AFK, I probably forgot to emit a BAK..." | discussion, correctly ignored |
| "afk since 13:30." | *retrospective report*, not a departure |

*That third rejection is right for a subtler reason and names a separate
channel.* "afk since 13:30" was sent *on returning* — it labels a gap that had
already happened. Under the imputation rule it correctly reads as presence, but
the information it carries about the *preceding* gap is dropped. So there are
two kinds of statement here: a *departure marker* that opens an absence, and a
*retrospective correction* that labels a past one. Only the first is needed for
the state machine; the second is a richer channel and a later question.

*So the mechanism is now viable end to end:* transcript join (28 of 28 exact)
supplies the prompts, sentence-boundedness supplies the markers, and the
imputation rule supplies the state transitions without depending on the user
remembering anything.

*The user pushed back 2026-09-01, correctly:* "afk since 13:30" followed by a
long gap could equally mean /"I forgot to say AFK and I'm still AFK for now"/ —
so classifying it as a return is not airtight. It is not, and the data agrees:
that prompt was followed by a *77-minute* gap.

*Two corrections to the entry above, both from measurement.*

- *A contaminated denominator.* The "5 of 8" was measured over raw =type:"user"=
  transcript entries — which include *stop-hook feedback*, injected in the user
  role. Using the queue's =resume= events as the spine instead gives *36 real
  prompts* against 45 raw entries; nine were not prompts at all. This is
  [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]]'s recorded trap ("276
  =type:"user"= entries, almost all tool results") walked into again, and the
  fix is to let the queue define what a prompt is rather than the transcript.
- *The disambiguation comes from the other end.* The gap after "afk since 13:30"
  *is* labelled — not by its opener but by its *closer*, an explicit "BAK". So
  classifying **gaps** using markers at *either* end is strictly better than
  tracking an AFK *state* from openers alone, and it is what makes an ambiguous
  opener harmless.

*But coverage is partial, and that is the honest bound.* Of this session's seven
longest inter-prompt gaps, markers label *three*: 193m by its opener, 74m by its
closer, 68m by its opener. Four are unlabelled, including one of 776m and two
around an hour. So markers do not replace the threshold; they *override* it
where present.

*Which is the right shape anyway, given the stated bar.* The threshold treats
every gap over 1200s identically. A marker adds discrimination exactly where the
threshold is guessing — and can contradict it in both directions, marking a
short gap as absence or a long one as presence. Aiming for airtight would mean
asking the user to annotate every gap, which is the clerical burden this whole
line of work exists to avoid.

*So: markers as evidence, threshold as fallback, and the residue stated rather
than hidden* — roughly half of long gaps carry no marker today, and the honest
report says which are labelled and which are inferred rather than presenting one
number for both.

CLOCK: [2026-09-01 Tue 12:15]--[2026-09-01 Tue 12:18] =>  0:03
- [2026-09-01 Tue 12:15]--[2026-09-01 Tue 12:18] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-09-01 Tue 12:26]--[2026-09-01 Tue 12:27] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-01 Tue 12:26]--[2026-09-01 Tue 12:27] assigned at review

*Raised by the user 2026-09-01, considering turning clocking off altogether:*
the metric they actually want is *the ratio of their own time — thinking,
prompting, reading — to the time the agent is working*, and their expectation is
that any automated approximation of their attention "is a dramatic underestimate
of what is happening in reality".

*The underestimate is real, and it is on the other side of the ledger.* What
undercounts is the *agent* attribution, not the human: a span reading =0:11=
writes =0:01=, measured repeatedly on 2026-08-31. The human's time is not
undercounted — it is *uncollected by choice*.

*Because the numerator is already recorded, on every turn, and then discarded.*
=resume= fires on =UserPromptSubmit=; =pause= fires when the turn ends. So a
=pause= -> =resume= gap is *exactly* reading the response, thinking, and typing
the next prompt. =--span-work-runs= extracts only the =resume= -> =pause= runs
and treats the gaps as idle.
[[id:44cef181-8dac-4735-8860-f123e132fdbc][44cef181]] already says so in as many
words: "the human reading a session report -- real attention, discarded by the
extraction rather than recorded as anything."

*Measured on this session's own queue, 2026-09-01:* 44 guideposts, 19 agent runs
and 19 human gaps.

| quantity | value |
|----------+-------|
| agent working (=resume= -> =pause=) | 2.75 h across 19 runs, median 353 s |
| human gaps (=pause= -> =resume=) | 19.81 h across 19 gaps, median 393 s, max 760 min |
| human gaps under 10 minutes | 1.08 h across 13 gaps |
| turns (=resume= events) | 22 |

*The raw ratio of 7.19 : 1 is meaningless* — it is dominated by six AFK gaps,
one of them 12.7 hours. Capped at ten minutes it is *0.39 : 1*. The true figure
is between them and only a human knows where.

*Which is the actual finding: the judgement needed is far smaller than the one
currently being asked for.* The review pass asks the human to attribute spans
*to headings*. This ratio needs no heading at all — it is session-global. The
only judgement is "were you at the keyboard", on *six* gaps today rather than
nineteen, and only for the raw total; the capped figure and the turn count need
none.

*So collection and attribution can be decoupled, and that is the proposal.*
Guideposts are written by hooks, cost nothing, and need no Emacs. Attribution to
headings is the expensive half and the one the user says they value least.
Turning off *attribution* while continuing to *collect* would preserve this
metric entirely and remove the clerical burden that prompted the question.

*What that would give up, stated so the trade is visible:* per-heading clock
intervals, and with them any answer to "how long did this task take".
[[id:4f8500e6-b468-4fd4-a0f6-ebf04c7a57ba][4f8500e6]] is the heading that says
constructing attention intervals is manual by nature; this does not change that,
it only observes that the *ratio* does not require it.

*Open, and genuinely undecided:* whether the gap-versus-AFK threshold should
ever be automated. [[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]] is the
standing precedent against an invented number, and a ten-minute cap is exactly
that. Reporting both bounds and letting the human place the line is the
alternative — and unlike the current review pass, it is six questions a day
rather than nineteen.

*The theme, briefly, in place of a plan — the full prompt comes later.* The
previous slice was about the gap between what the code *knows* and what it
*says*. This one is about a gap one layer down: the record itself going wrong
while every check reports success.

Three things happened on 2026-08-31/09-01 that share a shape. =bin/lint-org=
reported *0 errors* across several runs while a duplicate =:ID:= sat in the
corpus — and an =:ID:= is the primary key, so a duplicate makes every id-taking
tool ambiguous. The live Emacs served a =config.el= two hours stale, silently,
and was caught only because the change happened to be to *visible wording*; a
behavioural change would have been invisible. And the ceremony's archive step
failed *after* copying and before deleting, leaving a heading in two files at
once — a partially-applied write, not a refused one.

To which add the standing friction: the two MCP tools that time out are worked
around by =emacsclient= a dozen times a session, so the tool surface is bypassed
rather than fixed; and two =id= arguments reject an 8-character prefix, one of
them by *silently doing nothing* and reporting a clean no-op.

*So the members divide roughly three ways:* checks that do not catch, writes
that half-succeed, and one archive duplication whose mechanism is still unknown.
What they have in common is that none of them is visible from a green suite.

*Composition note:* the member list below is deliberately not sequenced yet, and
the incidental list is generated.

*=60ed5b96= added 2026-09-04, after composition.* It is the theme stated with
the nouns changed: =--review-format-annotation='s docstring says "Always
/inactive/ timestamps … and no branch to make" four lines above a =let*= that
branches. When it misled two readings in opposite directions the same day,
=bin/test= was 543/543, =bin/check-conventions= 27 ok and =bin/lint-org= 0
errors — the record wrong, every check green, which is this slice's sentence.

It belongs to the *checks that do not catch* third of the division above, and
for a reason worth keeping: =bin/check-conventions= compares the **documents**
against the code, and nothing compares a **docstring** against the function
beneath it. It also sits directly beside =f12f9da4=, which is the same shape one
layer out — a stale thing, silently authoritative, caught only because the drift
happened to be visible.

*Its sibling was considered and left out.* =33c3eabc= (=footnote-check= discards
the id it resolved) arrived the same hour and is **not** a member: that hook
fails *loudly*, which is the opposite of "none of them is visible from a green
suite". Note the two footnote headings therefore split across this boundary on
purpose — =2a6a1355= is a member because *missing* a footnote is invisible, and
=33c3eabc= is not because it is about the hook's response to catching one.


*Two incidentals removed 2026-09-08, on a rule the user set the same
day*: work fast-tracked and completed as an incidental of a slice *in
progress* belongs to that slice alone, and is removed from every other
open slice's list. =f1ff027e= and =d585d33e= were =ff7ccb2d='s
incidentals — its work, interleaved with its members — and this slice's
window claimed them too, reading 4/15 with all three X's borrowed.
Until the derivation itself learns the rule, a refresh of this slice
will re-add them; the implementation heading carries that warning.

*=53b0047d= added 2026-09-08, after composition*, placed beside
=2aeb65d6= because they are the same shape one layer apart: a second
copy of the truth, written by an ordinary action, announcing nothing.
=2aeb65d6= is two sessions sharing a git index; this is a file write
and a live Emacs buffer each holding what the other lacks. It earned
its place the same day by *happening* — a slice member line written to
disk while a review pass sat unsaved in the buffer, each side unique,
neither =revert-buffer= nor =C-x C-s= safe alone. Every check was
green throughout, which is this slice's sentence.

*=7ee3b71a= added 2026-09-15, after composition*, placed beside
=53b0047d= and =2aeb65d6= for the reason those two sit together: a write
by an ordinary action that destroys state and announces nothing.
=org_amend= with =replace== erases a slice's member checklist — the one
thing a slice declares, every other field being derived — because the
checklist is body prose and =replace== rewrites body prose wholesale.
It earned its place the same way =53b0047d= did, by *happening*: it
destroyed =35582d95='s membership during that slice's own composition,
in the documented order (add members, then write the body).

*It belongs to the writes-that-half-succeed third, and it needed an
argument to get there*, because =bin/lint-org= did catch it — which
looks like the opposite of this slice's sentence. The catch was luck: the
error came from the =:BLOCKER:= naming ids that were no longer members,
so it fired only because that slice *had* a blocker. A slice whose
members are all finished carries none (=0086614a= shrinks it away), and
the destruction would then leave cookie and blocker agreeing at empty
with nothing to object — reporting =[0/0]= as though the slice were
empty rather than broken, which is the failure this project has already
named as the worst direction.

*Two in one week, and neither a regression*, which is the part that
generalises past either: the fill sweep wrapped member lines and this
erased them, both existing writers meeting a slice member line for the
first time. A member line looks like ordinary body prose and nothing in
the text says it is owned. That argues for making the region
identifiable to any writer rather than adding a guard per writer.


* TODO Three new ways to owe a footnote and not see it: marker, list, quotation :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       2a6a1355-615f-4c2c-a8af-c9ae8dcda09b
:CREATED:  [2026-09-01 Tue 08:43]
:CATEGORY: Skill
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- [2026-09-01 Tue 08:39]--[2026-09-01 Tue 08:42] assigned at review
:END:

*Asked by the user 2026-09-01 after a third stop-hook refusal in one session:*
"check for others this session (same or different?)". *Different — all three.*

| # | id refused | what the id was doing |
|---+------------+-----------------------|
| 1 | =72463b68= | superscript ⁴ was reused for two ids; the marker existed, the footnote line was another heading's |
| 2 | eleven at once | enumerated in prose lists and a table — "phase 4 has =c60a1c53=, then =5f1068f9=/=33864a0f=" |
| 3 | =b36e6369= | inside a *quoted example* — ="add X to b36e6369"=, illustrating what a commit subject looks like |

*None of these is either recorded mode.*
[[id:d5490814-d174-480a-a9e7-b809f94d71f2][d5490814]] is *id-as-vocabulary*: the
token has become the name of a lesson, so it stops reading as a citation.
[[id:2758f3a0-3c47-405e-9662-a7669dcb60cc][2758f3a0]] is *decay on re-mention*:
cited correctly the first time, abbreviated after. In all three cases here the
id was neither a name nor a re-mention.

*The unifying observation, and it is sharper than "be careful".* The hook's test
is *does the response contain this token*. The writer's test is *am I referring
to a heading here*. Those agree only while an id appears **because** it refers.
They diverge whenever it appears for some other reason:

- *bookkeeping* — the id was cited, and the failure is in the numbering rather
  than the recognition (case 1). This is the one where the intent was entirely
  correct;
- *enumeration* — the id is an item in a list of work, where the eye reads a row
  rather than a reference (case 2);
- *quotation* — the id belongs to a string being *shown*, not to a sentence
  being *written* (case 3).

*So the three recorded modes now cover recognition, decay, and
reference-versus-appearance*, and only the last has a mechanical smell to it:
the hook already knows every id in the response and every footnote marker, so it
could say *which* marker is duplicated or missing rather than only which id is
unfootnoted. Case 1 would have been a one-line fix at the moment it happened.

*Worth noting what did not fail.* Every response carrying ids in *ordinary
prose* passed. The rule is not hard to follow when the id is doing the job the
rule was written for.

*Open: whether any of this is worth mechanising.* Against — the hook already
catches every instance, so nothing reaches the user, and three refusals in a
session of roughly forty responses is a low rate for a check whose whole purpose
is to be the backstop. For — case 1 is a *numbering* error the hook could
diagnose precisely, and cases 2 and 3 suggest the writer's attention is on the
sentence rather than the token, which no amount of care fixes. Deciding that is
this heading's job; it should not be assumed either way.

*A fourth mode, found 2026-09-01 immediately after filing the first three, and
it is the only one the hook cannot see.*

A response footnoted [[id:2758f3a0-3c47-405e-9662-a7669dcb60cc][2758f3a0]] as
"Footnote discipline decays on re-mention, not on first citation". Its actual
title is *"[1/1] Footnote discipline decays on re-mention; enforce it at Stop"*.
The wrong version was reconstructed from a *paraphrase inside another heading's
body* — [[id:d5490814-d174-480a-a9e7-b809f94d71f2][d5490814]] describes it as
"footnote discipline decaying on *re-mention*, where every offender was correct
the first time" — which reads exactly like a title and is not one.

*The hook passed it.* =footnote-check= tests whether each cited id *has* a
footnote, not whether the footnote is *right*. So this mode produces a
confidently-wrong citation that no mechanism refuses — the opposite of the other
three, where the failure is loud and the response is simply rejected.

*Which makes it the more serious of the four, on this project's own reasoning.*
A refused response costs a retry. A footnote with a plausible wrong title is
[[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]]'s shape — a value that is
harder to reject than an absence — and it propagates: the next reader has no
reason to doubt it, and the footnote exists precisely for readers who cannot see
the heading.

*The instruction already anticipates this and says so in as many words* — the
standing rule is that each footnote carries the id's "*exact, full title*,
looked up rather than recalled", and that "a remembered title is where
paraphrase creeps back in". What this instance adds is that *a paraphrase in a
sibling heading's body is indistinguishable from a lookup*, because it arrives
as prose about that heading and in this project's own voice. Recalling from
memory at least feels like recall; reading a neighbour's summary feels like
reading the source.

*So the mechanisable half moves.* The first three modes argue for better marker
bookkeeping in the hook. This one argues for something different and cheaper:
the hook already resolves every cited id to check it is tracked, so it *has* the
real title and could compare it against the footnote line. That would turn the
only silent mode of the four into a loud one.

*A fifth mode, 2026-09-01: the id arrives inside borrowed prose.* A response
compressed [[id:96a51c2f-ce11-4b40-bdff-11223d3d4eb7][96a51c2f]]'s own sentence
— "Since =226ed53b= the gap threshold governs only grouping and display" — into
its own words, and =226ed53b= came along as part of the transcription rather
than as a citation anyone chose to make. The hook refused it, twice in one
response.

*Distinct from the quotation mode above, and worse.* That one was an id inside a
*quoted example string*, visibly not the writer's own sentence. This one reads
as the writer's prose, because it is — only a fragment of it was copied. Nothing
on the surface marks the borrowed span, so there is no cue to check.

*Which makes the pattern across all five statable, and it is sharper than any of
them individually.* Every failure is an id the writer *did not choose to write*:
marker bookkeeping, list enumeration, quoted example, a title paraphrased from a
neighbour's body, and now a sentence adapted from one. The ids that are
deliberately cited get footnoted every time — that half of the discipline has
never failed.

*So the rule the writer is actually following is "footnote what I decided to
reference", and the hook's rule is "footnote every id present".* The gap between
those two is exactly the set of ids that arrive by transcription. That is a more
useful statement of the defect than five separate modes, and it suggests the
check to want is not better discipline but a diff: the hook already knows every
id in the response and every id in the footnote list.

*Asked 2026-09-01: could footnoting be done by a subagent? It should not, and
the reason is that most of it is already done by a script.*

*=bin/hooks/footnote-check= already performs every step but the last.* It
extracts the =\b[0-9a-f]{8}\b= candidates from the response, filters them by
grepping =^:ID: +$id= across TODO.org and DONE.org — so it *has located the
heading* — and checks which are missing from the footnote block. What it never
does is read the *title* sitting a few lines above the =:ID:= line it just
matched.

*Which makes the fix small and the model unnecessary.* Emitting id, current
keyword and exact title for each unfootnoted citation would make the fourth mode
— a title reconstructed from a neighbour's paraphrase — *structurally
impossible*, because the correct string would be on screen at the moment the
footnote is written. That is the only one of the five modes the hook cannot
currently catch, and it is the only one that produces a confidently wrong answer
rather than a rejected response.

*A subagent would be the wrong instrument for three reasons.* The task is a
lookup, not a judgement — there is nothing to weigh. It would be
non-deterministic where the answer is exact. And it could *paraphrase*, which is
precisely the failure being fixed; a =grep= cannot.

*The real constraint is what a hook may do, and it is worth stating because it
bounds every version of this.* A =Stop= hook can refuse and inform; it cannot
rewrite the response. So no mechanism here can *write* the footnotes — it can
only hand over the exact lines. That still removes two of the three error
classes: detection (the hook already sees every id) and lookup (it would now
carry the title). What remains is *placement* — the superscript markers — which
is where the marker-collision mode lives and which nothing but care can fix.

*So the honest scope: two of five modes become impossible, one becomes loud, and
two stay.* Enumeration and borrowed prose are already caught by the existing
check; the marker collision stays a hand error; the paraphrased title stops
being possible. That is a good return on adding one field to a message the hook
already prints.

*Refined 2026-09-01: not done by a subagent — assisted by one?* The distinction
is real, so the question is what a model could add that a script cannot.

*Almost nothing, on inspection.* Detection, lookup, presence-checking, ordering
by first appearance and comparing a written footnote against the true title are
all exact operations, and =footnote-check= already performs the first three. A
model would be slower, non-deterministic, and able to paraphrase — the failure
being fixed.

*One candidate is genuinely model-shaped, and it is the inverse of the rule the
hook enforces.* The hook asks "is every id present also footnoted". It cannot
ask "was every heading *discussed* also cited" — a response can describe a
heading at length, by subject rather than by id, and owe a footnote nobody can
detect by scanning for tokens. That is a judgement about aboutness, which is
exactly what a regex cannot do.

*Measured before proposing it, and the result is a clean negative.* Across *314
assistant responses* in this session, scanning for a heading whose four-word
title phrase appears while its id does not: *zero*. The failure this assist
would catch did not occur once.

*But the measurement cannot see the case the model would be best at, and that
limit is the finding.* The test requires a *verbatim* title fragment. Discussion
that paraphrases — "the ceremony trigger", "the read-only guard" — matches
nothing and is invisible to it. So the zero rules out verbatim uncited
discussion and says nothing about the paraphrased kind, which is precisely where
a model would earn its place. *Measuring that needs a different instrument, and
the obvious one is the model itself* — which makes the evidence circular unless
a human adjudicates a sample.

*So the ordering is clear even though the question is not settled.* Extend the
hook first: it is cheap, exact, and closes the only mode that currently produces
a wrong answer rather than a refusal. Revisit assistance only if an
uncited-by-paraphrase instance is ever *observed*, rather than building for a
failure with no recorded occurrence — which is
[[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]]'s standing argument, and
applies to a check as much as to a guess.


* TODO Report worked headings that carry no clock interval that day :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       37147e9e-468b-43ee-91f9-45d9647c6164
:CREATED:  [2026-09-01 Tue 08:38]
:CATEGORY: Clock
:END:

Split out of [[id:c60a1c53-ba7f-40a2-979a-6ce0a16ce3c8][c60a1c53]] 2026-09-01,
which shipped the slice-omission half and left this one in prose. That body
rates *this* the more valuable of the two, so leaving it as a paragraph inside a
closed heading was the wrong place for it.

*The signal already exists.* =claude-code-ide-org--commit-subject-ids= returns
the headings a commit subject claims to be *about*. Crossing that against the
day's clock intervals answers a second question from the same scan: which worked
headings carry no clocked time at all.

*Why it is the more valuable half, in that body's own words.* A forgotten slice
member is a bookkeeping error someone notices at review. *Un-clocked work is
invisible*: it lands on the meta-work day node as an unassigned span,
indistinguishable at review from planning, and the human attributing it has no
way to tell that three of those hours were implementation on a specific heading.

*Measured when it was found, 2026-08-25:* 52 state changes queued that day and
*zero* clock events, the last having been 2026-08-24 16:42. Every heading went
=TODO= -> =DONE= in one step — not a listed transition, so no rule ever fired to
open a clock. *The omission is structural rather than forgetful*: nothing asks
for a clock on a transition that was never made.

*And it is exactly the residue
[[id:eaeeb4ee-ceee-4094-8598-bbcdc8214b66][eaeeb4ee]] shipped to eliminate*,
which is the sharp part. That heading made explicit clock pairs authoritative so
attribution would stop needing a human — and it was the first member of the
slice during which the pairs stopped being produced at all. It closed the
consumption half; the discipline half regressed behind it.

*Open, and it is a different report with a different audience*, which is why it
was not smuggled in beside the forgotten-member line: that one asks "should this
be a slice member", this one asks "where did the day go". Worth deciding whether
it belongs at the end of a review pass at all, or in the =SessionStart= report
where the other attention questions live.

*Related:* [[id:445ec1f1-095a-462b-abbd-7a3a00925ddb][445ec1f1]] is the A/B on
whether the logged record means anything; this is one of the concrete gaps that
A/B would be measuring.


* TODO bin/lint-org does not detect a duplicate :ID: across TODO.org and DONE.org :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       39039bb6-c70a-4ba9-8b37-edd8dd6921f6
:CREATED:  [2026-09-01 Tue 08:19]
:CATEGORY: Dev
:END:

*Found 2026-09-01, by a corruption surviving every commit-time check.* A
partially-applied archive left
[[id:c31b6c76-8ad8-45d4-a87b-39e085e31368][c31b6c76]] as a heading in *both*
TODO.org and DONE.org ([[id:13ea6770-88ba-484d-87bd-2d20c44dc610][13ea6770]]),
and =bin/lint-org= reported *0 errors* throughout — across several runs and a
=.githooks/pre-commit= gate.

*Why this is a gap rather than an omission.* An =:ID:= is the primary key of
this entire system: [[id:478d6ec9-1b99-4c6c-88e9-c793ad4b2f22][478d6ec9]] made
prefixes resolvable precisely so ids could be referenced everywhere, and
=--id-find= resolves a prefix by scanning for a *unique* match. A duplicate id
therefore does not merely offend tidiness — it makes resolution *ambiguous*, and
every tool that takes an id can silently act on the wrong copy. The prefix
expander already has an =ambiguous= answer for the case where two ids share a
prefix; it has nothing for two headings sharing an id outright.

*It was caught, but by the wrong thing and too late.* The archive's own
post-condition reported =Archived 20, but 1 id(s) now appear twice in DONE.org=.
That guard fires *after* writing and does not roll back, so it documents a
corruption rather than preventing one. A lint rule runs before every commit and
would have refused it.

*Cheap, and the scan already exists.* =claude-code-ide-org--lint-heading-ids=
already walks every heading in every file collecting ids — the duplicate check
is a =seq-group-by= over what it has in hand rather than new traversal.

*Error rather than warning, on this project's own test:* the correct state is
computable and a duplicate is never a judgement call. Worth reporting *both*
locations, since "id X is duplicated" without saying where sends the reader
grepping — the failure [[id:6cc71c36-0fa6-4773-9b35-c0582f4cee82][6cc71c36]] is
about.

*One question to settle while building it:* whether an =:ID:= appearing in an
=:ARCHIVE_OLPATH:=-style property or in body prose should count. It should not —
only heading =:ID:= properties are keys — but the scan must be written to
distinguish them, and a naive =grep '^:ID:'= over file text would already be
right for the wrong reason.


* TODO The running Emacs can be running a stale config.el and nothing says so :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       f12f9da4-2870-45b7-8c50-ea54415c7bc6
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 17:47]
:CATEGORY: Dev
:END:

*Raised by the user 2026-08-31 after the live Emacs was found serving a
=config.el= roughly two hours out of date.* Their question was whether the SOP
needs invoking explicitly from the next-session prompt. It does not, and the
reason is the interesting part.

*The SOP exists, is thorough, and is in the right place.* The org-dev skill's
own description already says to trigger "proactively whenever =config.el=,
=config-test.el=, or the Doom config has just been edited in this session —
don't wait to be asked before checking that a code change actually loaded", and
§1 gives the =load-file= procedure, §2 the cases needing a full restart, §4 a
sanity-check pattern. *Nothing about the guidance failed. It was simply not
invoked*, across roughly fifteen edits to =config.el= in one session.

*So the gap is not documentation, and adding it to the next-session prompt would
be the wrong shape twice over.* That prompt is a *plan* — consumed and rewritten
once acted on — so a standing need placed there evaporates by construction. And
the instruction already sits where Claude Code actually matches it. Writing it a
second time creates a copy that can disagree with the first, which is the
objection this project raises against declaring anything already derivable.

*The measured part, because "this has not been a problem in recent sessions" was
the user's own framing and deserved testing rather than agreement.* =config.el=
churn per day over the preceding fortnight: 2026-08-24 was 16 commits and +1035
lines, 08-26 was 12 and +853, 08-25 was 11 and +849. This session was 16 commits
and +959 — *entirely typical*. So "recent sessions edited it less" is dead as an
explanation.

*Which leaves the uncomfortable hypothesis, and it is the one this heading is
really about: it has been happening and going unnoticed.* The failure is silent
by construction — a stale module produces no error, and the review pass simply
behaves as it did yesterday. Today it was caught by *luck of the change's
shape*: the edit happened to be to visible *wording* in a tool reply, and
=writes 0:05 in 1= was recognisable as a string that should no longer exist. The
behavioural changes shipped the same afternoon — auto-marking, the =g=
confirmation, the no-op skip — would all have been *invisible*, and an apply
pass using the old code would have looked entirely normal. Absence of reports is
therefore not evidence of absence.

*Precedent that it is a real failure mode rather than a worry:* the org-dev
skill records 2026-08-11, when the =:SESSIONS:= drawer was retired, every writer
deleted, all 49 drawers removed, suite green — and minutes later a clock-out
recreated one, because =config.el= had been edited on disk and never reloaded.

*Proposed fix: make the failure loud rather than the discipline stronger.* A
trigger that depends on model judgement has now failed silently for a whole
session, and the answer this project reaches for is a mechanism that reports
rather than a rule to remember. Sketch, and it is cheap:

- =config.el= sets a =claude-code-ide-org--loaded-at= timestamp at load time.
  Confirmed absent today, so this is a new =defvar= plus one =setq= at top
  level.
- Anything already round-tripping to Emacs compares it against
  =(file-attribute-modification-time (file-attributes config-path))= and says so
  when the file is newer.
- The natural home is the =SessionStart= report, which already carries the
  stale-interval and ceremony prompts and already follows the *ask, never act*
  contract. A second line there costs nothing.

*Deliberately not proposed: auto-reloading on edit.* It looks tidier and is
worse — §2 of the skill lists changes a live reload cannot cover, so an
automatic =load-file= would sometimes produce a *confident* wrong answer, which
is the failure mode this project consistently rates as worse than an absent one.

*Note the check has a real limit worth writing into it:* file mtime is newer
than load time is not the same as "the running Emacs is wrong", since an edit
may not have touched anything loaded. The report should say what it knows — the
file is newer than the load — and let the reader decide, exactly as the
stale-interval report states a timestamp and asks rather than guessing.


* TODO Sixty minutes holding 2 turns is not sixty holding 40 [2/7]
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       f90d745e-ccf9-49d6-a92e-df25c17b4dfb
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 16:39]
:CATEGORY: Queue
:END:

Clock duration cannot tell attention apart from absence. Sixty minutes holding 2
turns and sixty holding 40 are identical in a =:LOGBOOK:= and completely
different in what the human was doing — and attention, not duration, is this
project's second stated goal. Turn density is the cheapest available proxy:
=resume= events already exist, one per =UserPromptSubmit=, so every child below
is a *view* of data already recorded rather than new collection.

The children divide by *where the signal has to appear* and *what has to be true
before it means anything*. Two standing constraints govern all of them: **never
present a total that looks complete**, since the tool sees Claude sessions and
nothing else and the gaps are exactly where meatspace went; and **prefer the
lighter presentation every time**, because the point is to keep a human's
judgement calls quick, and a readout that is more accurate but heavier to read
is a worse readout.

*Born 2026-08-31 by dividing
[[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]]*, which had grown a
shipped half and four open ones inside a single body.


** DONE Show turn count on the span line, where the attention call is made :code:
CLOSED: [2026-08-31 Mon 16:43]
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Queue
:ID:       9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5
:CREATED:  [2026-08-15 Sat 14:07]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-08-15 Sat 14:04]--[2026-08-15 Sat 14:08] =>  0:04
- [2026-08-15 Sat 14:04]--[2026-08-15 Sat 14:08] assigned at review
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-15 Sat 14:07] \\
  surface undecided, analysis recorded
CLOCK: [2026-08-15 Sat 14:31]--[2026-08-15 Sat 14:33] =>  0:02
- [2026-08-15 Sat 14:31]--[2026-08-15 Sat 14:33] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-15 Sat 14:44]--[2026-08-15 Sat 14:45] =>  0:01
- [2026-08-15 Sat 14:44]--[2026-08-15 Sat 14:45] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-15 Sat 14:53]--[2026-08-15 Sat 14:58] =>  0:05
- [2026-08-15 Sat 14:53]--[2026-08-15 Sat 14:58] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-15 Sat 15:01]--[2026-08-15 Sat 15:03] =>  0:02
- [2026-08-15 Sat 15:01]--[2026-08-15 Sat 15:03] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-15 Sat 15:17]--[2026-08-15 Sat 15:31] =>  0:14
- [2026-08-15 Sat 15:17]--[2026-08-15 Sat 15:31] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-31 Mon 16:38]--[2026-08-31 Mon 16:43] =>  0:05
- State "DOING"      from "TODO"       [2026-08-31 Mon 16:38]
- [2026-08-31 Mon 16:38]--[2026-08-31 Mon 16:43] mitosis and prose redistribution
- State "DONE"       from "DOING"      [2026-08-31 Mon 16:43]
:END:
:PLAN:

Put the turn count on the review buffer's span line, so a human judging an
interval can see what the stretch *felt* like without doing arithmetic.

*Why the review line and not the durable record.* The first recommendation was
the opposite — annotation only, keep it out of the review buffer — on the
reasoning that =:LOGBOOK:= is what a human reads weeks later. That reversed once
it was settled that attention intervals are constructed *at review*: if the
judgement happens there, that is where the information has to be. The annotation
keeps a narrower job and is
[[id:95755305-0499-4022-bd75-f325fd6828c7][95755305]].

*Count =resume=, not =pause=.* An interrupted turn emits no =pause= but always
had a =resume=, so the resume is the robust anchor.

*A recall cue, not a metric.* The value of turn density is that it tells the
human what a stretch felt like — at the keyboard, or set running and left —
quickly enough that they recognise it and move on. A number they must weigh is
heavier than a word they can recognise, and lightness wins.

---

:END:
*Shipped 2026-08-31.* The span line reads =0:11 span, 0:01 in 1 run, 6 turns=.
Turn count is the third of three observed quantities, built in one pass with
[[id:98c302e0-ab1b-40bf-92d0-d2f2e16ac05d][98c302e0]] and
[[id:44cef181-8dac-4735-8860-f123e132fdbc][44cef181]] because all three bodies
asked for that rather than three rewordings of one line.

*No new data, exactly as predicted.* =claude-code-ide-org--span-turn-seconds=
already returned =(SECONDS . TURNS)=; the count was computed and discarded. The
"a view of data already recorded" argument held with nothing to add — no hook,
no payload field, no failure mode.

*The foundation was checked before building on it, and the check produced a trap
worth keeping.* Measured 2026-08-15: 22 =resume= events against ~25
string-content user messages in that session's transcript. Close but not equal,
and the discrepancy is systematic rather than random — harness-injected
user-role turns (=ToolSearch= "Tool loaded" replies, =/plan= output) and
mid-turn interjections. *And the transcript holds 276 =type:"user"= entries,
almost all tool results*: a naive count of user messages is off by an order of
magnitude.

*The classifier was deliberately not built.* A dense/sparse label would have
satisfied the recall-cue requirement more literally, but it needs a threshold,
and a threshold is an invented number laid on a measured one — against which
this story's own objection stands. Juxtaposition carries it: =6 turns= beside
=0:11 span= reads as "at the keyboard" with nothing invented. The interpretive
word is deferred, not rejected, and is a one-line addition if wanted.

*Verified:* the suite went 465 to 468 tests, 0 unexpected, exit 0, with the
readout rework. Fixing the turn count to a constant fails three tests and
nothing else.

*Left to siblings rather than done here, and each is now a heading rather than a
bullet:* the =:LOGBOOK:= annotation
([[id:95755305-0499-4022-bd75-f325fd6828c7][95755305]]), the clocktable column
([[id:97503194-5ef3-4a1e-b25d-676c3cb39eb0][97503194]]), the cross-session
allocation problem ([[id:b862fbf4-53b8-48b5-b773-1c7c0332dfb4][b862fbf4]]),
where the not-productivity caveat lives
([[id:c4bc5c70-861a-4ce0-80da-c79b6b1600f3][c4bc5c70]]), and the zero-turns
ambiguity for background and subagent sessions
([[id:9b5eb830-8825-4d6c-b701-c167c2d71fdf][9b5eb830]]).

*One thing this heading shipped without:* nothing on the line says the number
must never be read as productivity, which under autonomy it inverts. That is
[[id:c4bc5c70-861a-4ce0-80da-c79b6b1600f3][c4bc5c70]], and it is a real gap
rather than a deferral — the argument that juxtaposition carries the meaning
covers a reader building an impression, not one importing a reading from
elsewhere.

Split out of [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]] 2026-08-31,
which shipped the review-buffer surface and named this as still open.

The turn count now appears on the review line, but queue files are drained and
archived, and =:LOGBOOK:= is what a human reads weeks later. Not writing it
there means re-deriving it from archives — which is possible and is exactly the
friction the count exists to remove.

=claude-code-ide-org--review-format-annotation= is the site. The parent's
original judgement was "costs one token on a line that already exists", which
still looks right.

*Open question inherited rather than settled:* whether the annotation should
carry the *envelope* too, now that the review line reports envelope, runs and
turns together. An annotation recording only the runs loses the same distinction
[[id:44cef181-8dac-4735-8860-f123e132fdbc][44cef181]] was filed about, one layer
down and where nobody is watching.

*Analysis inherited from [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]]
on 2026-08-31, boiled down from where it sat among three competing surfaces.*

*This was originally judged the most useful of the three, and then demoted — the
reversal is the part worth carrying.* The first recommendation was "annotation
only, keep it out of the review buffer", on the reasoning that the durable
record is the point: queue files are drained and archived, and =:LOGBOOK:= is
what a human reads weeks later, so not writing it there means re-deriving it
from archives.

That was reversed once it was settled that the human constructs attention
intervals *at review*. If the judgement happens at review, that is where the
information has to be — which is why the review line shipped first. *The
annotation's job is therefore narrower than it looked:* not to inform the call,
but to record enough of the outcome and its evidence that the call can be
revisited later without being redone.

*Cost is one token on a line that already exists* —
=claude-code-ide-org--review-format-annotation=.

*Open question this heading now owns.* The review line reports *three*
quantities — envelope, runs, turns — because a span's outer bracket is not what
it writes. An annotation recording only the runs loses exactly that distinction,
one layer down and where nobody is watching, which is
[[id:44cef181-8dac-4735-8860-f123e132fdbc][44cef181]] recurring in the durable
record instead of the buffer. So: does the annotation carry the envelope and the
turn count too, and at what cost to a line that has to stay readable inside a
drawer?

gap lists cannot be summed at all, and turns/hour can be averaged across a week.
Aggregate post-hoc analysis is where =org_clock_report= lives, so it is the one
